A family’s childcare arrangement can matter as much as its choice of neighbourhood.\n\nThe country landing page is [Finorum Cost of Living in the Netherlands](https://finorum.com/cost-of-living-in-the-netherlands/).\n\n## Estimated Monthly Budget\n\nThe following ranges describe complete, modest-to-moderate household scenarios. Replace their rent with an actual offer and adjust the remaining lines, rather than adding rent to a total that already includes it.\n\n## Cost of Housing and Rent\n\n### What the market figures describe\n\nPararius reported a **€1,882 average monthly rent and €20.94 per square metre for newly let unregulated homes in Q2 2026**. Its dataset covers lettings recorded through participating professional market channels; it does not represent all existing Dutch tenancies. The report recorded 761 more withdrawals than new listings and more than 41% of available homes above €2,000 monthly. Listing withdrawals are not, by themselves, proof that every withdrawn home was sold. [Pararius Q2 2026 rental report](https://www.pararius.com/news/sell-off-of-rental-homes-in-the-netherlands-spread).\n\nA portal’s advertised rent is an asking price. An agreed rent after letting is a transaction observation. Neither tells you what a long-standing tenant in a regulated home pays. Check the dwelling and contract before deciding which market comparison applies.\n\n### Private-rental planning ranges\n\nThese are broad search allowances for independent accommodation, informed by the market’s city differences. They are **not measured centre-versus-suburb averages**. They assume basic rent excluding energy, water, service charges and local taxes. Smaller or older homes are more likely to fit the lower ends; furnished, renovated or particularly well-located homes may exceed the upper ends.\n\n| Location | Central studio / one bedroom | Outside centre, studio / one bedroom | Central two–three bedrooms | Outside centre, two–three bedrooms |\n| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |\n| Amsterdam | €1,750–2,600 | €1,450–2,200 | €2,600–3,800+ | €2,150–3,200 |\n| Rotterdam | €1,400–2,050 | €1,150–1,700 | €2,000–2,850 | €1,650–2,450 |\n| The Hague | €1,350–2,000 | €1,100–1,700 | €1,950–2,900 | €1,650–2,450 |\n| Utrecht | €1,450–2,150 | €1,200–1,800 | €2,100–3,100 | €1,750–2,600 |\n| Eindhoven | €1,250–1,850 | €1,050–1,600 | €1,850–2,700 | €1,550–2,300 |\n| Regional alternative, such as Enschede and surrounding towns | €950–1,450 | €800–1,200 | €1,350–2,000 | €1,150–1,750 |\n\nThe low end of a regional range can overlap the regulated sector. An advertisement calling a property “private” does not establish that its rent is legally unregulated. Nor does a high asking price override a points-based maximum.\n\nFor context, Pararius’s Q2 2026 city figures were €28.69/m² in Amsterdam, €22.79 in Rotterdam, €22.42 in The Hague, €21.61 in Utrecht, €20.25 in Eindhoven, €14.95 in Enschede and €15.21 in Zwolle. City averages reflect different mixes of properties; multiplying one by the floor area of a specific home is not a valuation. [Pararius](https://www.pararius.com/news/sell-off-of-rental-homes-in-the-netherlands-spread).\n\n### Regulated, middle and unregulated rent\n\nFor contracts starting in 2026, the government lists **€932.93 monthly basic rent** as the social-sector starting-rent boundary and **€1,228.07** as the upper middle-rent boundary. The dwelling’s points matter: the middle segment includes 144–186 points, while a new unregulated tenancy requires at least 187 points and a starting rent above the applicable boundary. Older contracts need assessment under the rules applicable to them. [Government explanation of rental sectors](https://www.rijksoverheid.nl/vraag-en-antwoord/huurwoning-zoeken/wat-is-het-verschil-tussen-een-sociale-huurwoning-een-middenhuurwoning-en-een-huurwoning-in-de-vrije-sector).\n\nSocial housing is not a dependable immediate relocation solution. Allocation conditions and local waiting systems apply. If you already have a regulated tenancy, use your own rent and contract when budgeting; newcomer market estimates may substantially overstate your housing costs.\n\nIn 2026 the general annual increase ceiling is 4.4% for unregulated rent and 6.1% for middle rent. For social housing, the usual ceiling from 1 July is 4.1%, with separate fixed-amount rules for particular low-rent or higher-income situations. A ceiling is not an automatic entitlement to charge that increase: the contract and other legal limits still matter. [Government rent-increase rules](https://www.rijksoverheid.nl/vraag-en-antwoord/woning-huren/welke-regels-gelden-er-voor-een-huurverhoging), [2026 middle-rent ceiling](https://www.rijksoverheid.nl/vraag-en-antwoord/woning-huren/maximale-huurverhoging-middenhuur-2026).\n\n### Contract, furnishing and application costs\n\nPermanent contracts have been the norm for new residential lettings since 1 July 2024, with defined exceptions. Do not assume every landlord can still issue an ordinary two-year temporary contract. Student or other specialist arrangements need their own assessment. [Government tenancy-contract guidance](https://www.rijksoverheid.nl/vraag-en-antwoord/woning-verhuren/moet-ik-een-huurcontract-voor-onbepaalde-tijd-aanbieden).\n\nFor new contracts since 1 July 2023, a deposit may not exceed **two months of basic rent**. It is refundable security rather than an additional month’s living expense, although you need the cash upfront. Record the condition of the property, meter readings and inventory at handover. [Government deposit guidance](https://www.rijksoverheid.nl/vraag-en-antwoord/woning-huren/woning-huren-waarborgsom).\n\nAn agent acting for the landlord cannot also charge the tenant a mediation fee for the same letting. Hiring your own independent search agent is a different arrangement. Ask who commissioned the agent and what any invoice actually covers. [ACM ConsuWijzer on letting-agent fees](https://www.consuwijzer.nl/nieuws/bemiddelingskosten-betaald-aan-uw-huurbemiddelaar-meld-het-bij-consuwijzer).\n\nPrepare identification, proof of income, an employment contract or employer statement, and any requested references. A landlord’s affordability test is separate from the amount you consider manageable. For example, a three-times-gross-income test on a €1,800 basic rent means €5,400 gross household income per month, if combined incomes are accepted. This is an illustration of a screening condition, not a legal national income requirement.\n\nAsk whether “unfurnished” means an empty shell or a home with flooring, curtains and appliances. Inspect the inventory for a furnished property. Furniture may reduce setup spending but increase the monthly amount. Also check:\n\n## Utilities and Household Bills\n\nA low rent can be offset by an inefficient heating system or substantial service charges. Electricity, gas or heat, drinking water and water-board taxes are separate items unless the agreement expressly combines them.\n\nThese **monthly planning allowances** describe a modest one- or two-adult apartment with ordinary consumption. They are not regulated tariffs.\n\n| Bill | Monthly allowance | Billing and interpretation |\n| --- | --- | --- |\n| Electricity, excluding electric space heating | €35–70 | Consumption plus allocated fixed charges; use the supplier’s complete quote |\n| Gas heating and hot water | €80–170 | Annualised allowance; winter consumption is higher |\n| District heating, if used instead of gas | €90–190 | Alternative to the gas line, not an additional full heating bill |\n| Drinking water | €15–30 | Metered supply, fixed charges and applicable taxes |\n| Municipal waste | About €25–45 | Often an annual assessment; municipality and household size matter |\n| Water-board charges | About €20–45 | Separate from drinking water; regional and household rules apply |\n| Tenant service charges | €40–100 | Shared cleaning, lighting and other contracted services; check whether heat is included |\n| Fixed internet | €35–60 | Provider, speed, address and post-promotion price matter |\n| SIM-only mobile | €8–20 per adult | Handset excluded |\n| Contents and personal liability insurance | €10–25 per household | Illustrative quote allowance; cover and excess vary |\n\nDo not add both heating alternatives. An all-electric home requires an electricity budget that includes heating; the €35–70 electricity range alone would be unsuitable. Nor should a tenant add a landlord’s whole building-owner association fee without checking what can properly be charged as tenant services.\n\nThe ACM’s **2026 maximum variable heat tariff is €40.97 per GJ** for supply within the relevant regulated framework. Fixed charges, metering and delivery-set costs can also apply, so multiplying consumption by that figure does not produce the complete annual bill. Ask for the previous annual statement and adjust for your occupancy, thermostat setting and contract. A larger, poorly insulated family home can exceed the table considerably.\n\n### Local charges: an Amsterdam example\n\nFor 2026, Amsterdam’s waste charge is **€352 annually for a one-person household** and **€469 for a household of two or more**. In the Amstel, Gooi en Vecht water-board area, the corresponding renter examples are **€279.64 and €465.22 annually** for resident and wastewater-treatment charges. [Amsterdam 2026 tax rates](https://www.amsterdam.nl/belastingen/belastingtarieven-2026/), [AGV 2026 water-board charges](https://www.agv.nl/waterschapsbelasting/tarieven/tarieven-waterschapsbelasting/).\n\nThe municipal sewer charge and ownership taxes depend on the municipality and liable party. A residential tenant should not automatically copy an owner-occupier’s tax assessment into a rental budget. Conversely, omitting the water-board bill because water is “included” in rent can understate costs.\n\n### Internet, mobile and insurance\n\nCompare broadband over the entire contract, including activation and the regular price after any discount. Address-specific availability matters; a provider’s national headline offer may not be available in the building. [KPN’s fibre availability and packages](https://www.kpn.com/internet/glasvezel?page=1).\n\nAs a current retail example, Ben lists monthly-cancellable SIM-only packages starting at €9.50, with a €20 connection charge for new customers on the published page. Bundle size and calling allowance affect the final price. This is a provider example, not an endorsement or the national average. [Ben monthly-cancellable SIM-only terms](https://www.ben.nl/sim-only/maandelijks-opzegbaar).\n\nContents cover protects your possessions; personal liability cover serves a different purpose. Obtain a quote for the correct address and household, and check whether a student policy already includes either. The allowance above excludes car insurance, expensive bicycles and specialist valuables.\n\n## Food and Grocery Costs\n\nFor groceries prepared mainly at home, use approximately **€300–450 monthly for one adult**, **€500–700 for a couple**, and **€750–1,000 for two adults with two school-age children**. A disciplined student can plan around €230–300. These editorial allowances exclude restaurant meals, household cleaning products and alcohol-heavy shopping.\n\nThe compact basket below is a shopping reference, not a complete nutritional basket or a national price survey. Exact examples were checked for this September update; broad ranges allow for product grade, brand and shop.\n\n| Item | Practical price reference | Unit and qualification |\n| --- | --- | --- |\n| Fresh semi-skimmed milk | €1.29 | 1 litre, AH product example |\n| Ordinary supermarket bread | About €1.50–3.00 | One full loaf; specialty bakery bread can cost more |\n| Barn eggs | €4.09 for 10; €4.91 equivalent for 12 | AH M/L eggs; the twelve-egg amount is a conversion, not a sold pack |\n| Basic dry spaghetti | €0.45 | 500 g AH product example |\n| Rice | About €1.50–3.50 | 1 kg; ordinary supermarket range |\n| Chicken breast | €4.49 for 300 g; about €14.97/kg | AH free-range product example; not all chicken is this price |\n| Gouda-style cheese | About €10–16 | 1 kg equivalent; block, slices and maturity affect price |\n| Apples or bananas | About €1.50–3.50 | 1 kg; seasonal and brand variation |\n| Carrots, onions or similar staple vegetables | About €1–3 | 1 kg; not a price for all vegetables |\n\nThe directly observed items are linked to [AH milk](https://www.ah.nl/producten/product/wi1525/ah-halfvolle-melk), [AH eggs](https://www.ah.nl/producten/product/wi1530/ah-verse-scharreleieren-l), [AH spaghetti](https://www.ah.nl/producten/product/wi62028/ah-spaghetti) and the [AH Dutch-product selection containing the chicken offer](https://www.ah.nl/shop/lekker-uit-nederland). The other rows are rounded planning allowances, not individually verified national averages.\n\nAlbert Heijn, Jumbo, PLUS, Lidl, Aldi and Dirk provide useful comparison points. Own-label staples and larger packs can help, but only if you use the contents before they spoil.\n\nNeighbourhood markets can be useful for produce and cheese; compare quality and price per kilogram. Imported favourites, organic products, prepared meals, station shops and small convenience branches can materially increase the bill. Check whether a promotion needs a loyalty account or requires buying more than you need.\n\nThe grocery budgets assume drinking water at home is paid through the household water bill. Bottled drinks are optional spending. Cleaning products, toiletries, nappies and pet food need their own allowance; a grocery-only figure should not silently be treated as the whole supermarket bill.\n\n## Restaurants, Cafés and Eating Out\n\nRestaurant prices vary more by location and format than supermarket staples. A lunch sandwich, a plated main and a complete dinner are different purchases.\n\n| Purchase | September 2026 planning range |\n| --- | --- |\n| Simple sit-down meal or substantial lunch | €15–25 per person |\n| Mid-range dinner for two | €70–110 including modest drinks; more for several courses |\n| Coffee | €2.50–4.50 |\n| Small draught beer, roughly 250–330 ml | €3.50–6 |\n| Soft drink | €3–4.50 |\n| Takeaway meal | €10–18 per person, before delivery and extras |\n\nFor a concrete comparison, Loetje’s displayed menu includes sandwiches around €12–15 and a small tenderloin main at €26.50 before separately ordered sides. Amsterdam café ’t Loosje lists espresso at €3.50, Coca-Cola Zero at €3.90 and a small Heineken at €3.70. These venue prices support the order of magnitude, not a national average. [Loetje menu](https://www.loetje.nl/en/menu/), [’t Loosje menu](https://www.loosje.nl/menu/).\n\nTipping is discretionary; rounding up or adding something for good service is a personal choice. Check the bill before adding a further amount where a service or group charge has been disclosed. Do not build an automatic large percentage into every Dutch restaurant purchase.\n\nFor budgeting, count occasions. Four €20 meals and eight €3.50 coffees cost €108 before any other outings. Keeping restaurant spending separate from groceries makes this visible and avoids overstating what the food-at-home allowance covers.\n\nA basic gym or local sports allowance of roughly €25–50 monthly can fit inside the leisure lines below. Treat this as a quote allowance: some providers bill every four weeks, creating thirteen payments per year, and may charge joining fees. Premium studios, swimming lessons and several children’s clubs require more.\n\n## Public Transport\n\nDutch cities combine buses, trams, metro services and rail with extensive opportunities to cycle. NS train subscriptions and municipal operator products cover different networks. There is no single cheap monthly ticket that automatically includes every journey nationwide.\n\n| Official product checked for 2026 | Price | Main limitation |\n| --- | --- | --- |\n| Amsterdam GVB one-hour ticket | €3.40 | GVB network and product validity; not an NS train ticket |\n| Amsterdam GVB day ticket | €10 | GVB services under the ticket conditions |\n| GVB Vrij monthly adult subscription | €129 | Operator-specific unlimited travel |\n| The Hague HTM two-hour ticket | €4.85 | HTM bus and tram validity |\n| NS Flex Dal Vrij, second class | €127.95/month | Unlimited eligible off-peak and weekend train travel |\n| NS Flex Dal Vrij, age 65+, second class | €59.95/month | Age and product conditions apply |\n| NS Flex Altijd Vrij, second class | €399.95/month | Train travel at all times; urban networks are separate |\n\nUse the official [GVB price list](https://www.gvb.nl/tarieven), [HTM fare list](https://www.htm.nl/reisproducten/producten-tarieven/) and [NS consumer rates](https://www.ns.nl/en/service-and-contact/payment/consumer-rates) to check the exact validity before purchase.\n\n**The €49 Nederland Dal Vrij summer offer is closed to new orders.** Its ordering window ended on 31 July 2026. Some customers who ordered during the window may still be within their individual promotional period, but a new September budget must not assume that price is available. [NS promotion conditions and status](https://www.ns.nl/en/travel/nederland-dal-vrij).\n\nContactless pay-as-you-go can suit occasional travel. Use the same payment medium to check in and out, and follow the operator’s instructions when changing networks. Compare actual trips in the journey planner before buying a subscription; a full monthly pass may be poor value for someone cycling most days.\n\nFor an intercity commute, price the specific route, peak/off-peak pattern and local connecting journey. Employer reimbursement should be counted only after the policy is confirmed. The transport lines in the worked budgets allow local travel and cycling, not an unrestricted peak-hour national rail subscription.\n\nSchiphol has rail connections, but an ordinary GVB ticket does not cover an NS airport train. Price the complete airport-to-home journey through the relevant operator; taxis and late-night alternatives need a separate allowance.\n\nStudents should not assume that enrolment alone brings free travel. The student travel product is tied to DUO eligibility and its conditions, including nationality or residence and, in some cases, employment. [DUO student-finance eligibility](https://www.duo.nl/particulier/student-finance/eligibility.jsp). Youth, senior and local low-income discounts also differ by product.\n\nFor cycling, budget for locks, lights, repairs and secure storage. A modest monthly maintenance reserve is more realistic than treating a bicycle as permanently free after purchase.\n\n## Driving and Car Ownership\n\nThe latest daily petrol observation verified for this update is **€2.466 per litre for Euro 95 on 7 September 2026**, published by CBS on 10 September. It is a weighted national pump-price average including VAT and excise, not a motorway-station quote or a price guaranteed for today. The earlier July petrol and diesel quotations have been removed from the current-price summary. [CBS daily motor-fuel prices](https://www.cbs.nl/nl-nl/cijfers/detail/80416ned?q=benzine).\n\nAt that petrol price, 800 km a month in a car using 6 litres per 100 km means 48 litres, or approximately **€118 in fuel**. This is a calculation, not an average Dutch driving pattern.\n\n| Recurring car item | Illustrative monthly allowance |\n| --- | --- |\n| Petrol under the example above | €118 |\n| Insurance | €50–110 |\n| Motor-vehicle tax | €35–90 for a modest petrol-car planning case |\n| Maintenance, tyres and inspection reserve | €60–110 |\n| Parking | €0–150+, depending heavily on address |\n| Depreciation or replacement saving | €100–250 |\n\nTogether, that scenario is around **€365–830 monthly**. It is an editorial model; insurance and tax must be quoted for the actual vehicle, driver and province. A heavier car, diesel vehicle, young driver, financed purchase or expensive city garage can exceed it. The family budget later in the guide includes no car.\n\nA car generally needs third-party liability insurance (WA) from registration in your name, applicable motor-vehicle tax and a valid APK inspection. Inspection timing depends on the vehicle; use the registration record rather than assuming every car follows the same schedule. Garages set their inspection prices, and repair work is additional. [Government car-purchase obligations](https://www.rijksoverheid.nl/vraag-en-antwoord/auto/auto-kopen), [RDW APK guidance](https://www.rdw.nl/apk/over-de-apk).\n\nThe purchase price, registration transfer, any import charges and initial repairs are one-time acquisition costs. Do not add the whole purchase price to a recurring monthly model that already includes depreciation. A finance repayment needs separate cash-flow treatment.\n\nThere is no general passenger-car motorway vignette, but particular toll crossings exist. On the **A24 near Rotterdam**, e-TOL requires electronic payment; without automatic payment, pay within three days after the journey. The absence of toll booths does not mean the road is free. [Official e-TOL guidance](https://www.e-tol.nl/).\n\nCheck resident parking eligibility before choosing a car or home. An address may have restrictions or a waiting list, and a permit is not a reserved parking space. Low-emission access is a separate question: Amsterdam’s passenger-car zone inside the A10 generally excludes diesel cars of emission class 4 or below, subject to exceptions. Do not apply commercial-van zero-emission rules indiscriminately to private cars. [Amsterdam vehicle-zone guidance](https://www.amsterdam.nl/en/traffic-transport/low-emission-zone/).\n\n## Healthcare Costs\n\nDutch healthcare combines compulsory insurance with tax and contribution funding. For most people who become subject to the Dutch system through living or working here, an individual basic policy is required. It is not covered simply by paying ordinary wage tax.\n\nArrange applicable Dutch cover promptly and within the four-month enrolment period; the obligation begins when your insured status starts, not four months later. Cross-border workers, treaty-covered pensioners and other exceptions need their status checked. [Government guidance for arrivals](https://www.government.nl/faq/health-insurance/when-do-i-need-to-take-out-health-insurance-if-i-come-to-live-in-the-netherlands).\n\nFor 2026, a useful concrete premium reference is **CZ Zorg-op-maat at €159.99 per adult per month with the €385 compulsory annual deductible**. Other policies differ in price and reimbursement for non-contracted providers. The worked resident budgets round this example to €160; it is not an official national mean premium. [CZ 2026 basic policies](https://www.cz.nl/zorgverzekering/basisverzekering).\n\n| Cost | How to budget |\n| --- | --- |\n| Adult basic premium | About €160/month in this guide’s examples; replace with your policy |\n| Mandatory deductible | €385 per adult per calendar year in 2026 |\n| Deductible reserve | €32.08/month funds the full annual amount; this is saving, not a monthly bill |\n| Children under 18 | No basic-policy premium or compulsory deductible; enrolment is still necessary |\n| Adult dentistry and additional cover | Separate allowance or policy according to expected treatment |\n\nThe deductible does not apply to every service. GP consultations are generally exempt, but tests, medicines or referred treatment can trigger deductible costs. Statutory co-payments are a different mechanism and can apply in addition. [Government deductible explanation](https://www.rijksoverheid.nl/vraag-en-antwoord/zorgverzekering/eigen-risico-zorgverzekering), [insurance obligations and children](https://www.rijksoverheid.nl/vraag-en-antwoord/zorgverzekering/ben-ik-verplicht-een-zorgverzekering-af-te-sluiten).\n\nRegister with a local GP and check the insurer’s provider network, particularly if you already need specialist treatment. Routine adult dental care is generally outside the basic package, whereas children have broader dental coverage; treatment-specific exceptions apply. Supplementary insurance can help with selected costs but is not a guarantee that every treatment is reimbursed. [Government health-insurance questions](https://www.government.nl/faq/health-insurance/more-qas-about-health-insurance-in-the-netherlands).\n\nA higher voluntary deductible can reduce the premium but increases the amount you may have to pay. The standard resident examples do not assume this trade-off. Their additional €50 per adult monthly reserve covers the compulsory deductible provision plus a modest amount for other out-of-pocket care; substantial dentistry or ongoing therapy needs more.\n\nHealthcare benefit, or **zorgtoeslag**, depends on qualifying Dutch insurance, income, assets and household circumstances. The 2026 individual income ceiling is €40,857; partner rules and asset tests also apply. It is not an automatic discount built into the insurer’s invoice. [Dienst Toeslagen eligibility](https://www.belastingdienst.nl/wps/wcm/connect/nl/zorgtoeslag/content/kan-ik-zorgtoeslag-krijgen).\n\n### Students and cross-border arrangements\n\nAn international student present solely for study is normally not automatically insured under the Dutch Wlz system and usually should not take out ordinary Dutch basic insurance. Paid work or an internship can change that position. Ask SVB for an assessment when unclear. [SVB student and internship rules](https://www.svb.nl/en/the-wlz-scheme/insurance-under-the-wlz-scheme/you-are-a-student-or-doing-an-internship).\n\nA valid EHIC can support eligible medically necessary care during a temporary stay under the responsible country’s rules. It is not a substitute for mandatory Dutch insurance when that obligation applies, nor comprehensive cover for repatriation or all private treatment. Study-only students should verify home-country coverage and any supplementary student policy rather than blindly using the adult resident premium. [Government EHIC guidance](https://www.government.nl/themes/family-health-and-care/health-insurance/standard-health-insurance/applying-for-a-european-health-insurance-card-ehic).\n\n## Childcare and Education\n\n### Daycare and after-school care\n\nChildcare is not automatically free. Providers charge hourly or package rates, and eligible parents may receive income-dependent childcare benefit. Check whether the provider and location are registered and whether your household meets the work, study or other qualifying conditions.\n\nFor 2026, the maximum hourly amounts used to calculate benefit are:\n\n| Type of care | Maximum eligible hourly rate for benefit calculation |\n| --- | --- |\n| Daycare centre | €11.23 |\n| After-school care, BSO | €9.98 |\n| Registered childminder, gastouder | €8.49 |\n\nThese are **subsidy calculation caps, not limits on what providers may charge**. Any excess above the cap remains with the parent. [Dienst Toeslagen maximum childcare rates](https://www.belastingdienst.nl/wps/wcm/connect/bldcontentnl/belastingdienst/prive/toeslagen/kinderopvangtoeslag/hoeveel-kinderopvangtoeslag-kan-ik-krijgen/maximaal-uurtarief-voor-de-kinderopvang).\n\nAt the highest 2026 reimbursement tier, qualifying households can receive 96% of eligible costs. Lower reimbursement percentages apply at higher incomes, and eligible hours and other conditions also matter. [Government childcare-benefit amounts for 2026](https://www.rijksoverheid.nl/themas/belastingen-uitkeringen-en-toeslagen/kinderopvangtoeslag/bedragen-kinderopvangtoeslag-2026).\n\nFor illustration, assume **100 billed daycare hours at a quoted €12/hour**. The invoice is €1,200. At a 96% reimbursement rate, benefit would be €1,078.08 on the €1,123 eligible amount, leaving €121.92. Without eligibility, the household pays the full €1,200. This hypothetical provider quote illustrates the calculation; it is not a verified national daycare price.\n\nAsk whether the contract includes holiday weeks, long opening-day blocks, meals and a minimum number of days. Multiply billed hours, not only the time the child is physically present. A place close to home may be impractical if its pickup time conflicts with the commute.\n\nMunicipally supported preschool places and programmes can have different conditions from ordinary daycare. Obtain a written assessment rather than assuming the childcare-benefit formula applies to every arrangement.\n\n### Ordinary school and international school\n\nParents do not pay tuition for publicly funded Dutch primary school. Voluntary contributions for extra activities must remain voluntary; children cannot be excluded from those activities because the contribution is unpaid. Lunch supervision and before- or after-school care can create separate charges. [Government primary-school cost guidance](https://www.rijksoverheid.nl/vraag-en-antwoord/basisonderwijs/kosten-basisschool-kind).\n\nThe family model allows €150 monthly for two children’s school-related incidentals and activities, with packed-lunch ingredients already in groceries. It does not imply a compulsory school fee. Older pupils can need a different allowance for travel, equipment and activities.\n\nInternational schools vary substantially. Government-supported international departments and independent schools do not share one fee schedule. As a high-cost, independent-school example, the **International School of Amsterdam lists €27,710 per child for Grades 1–5 in 2026–27**, excluding other applicable charges. Two children at that tuition level alone would require €55,420 annually, or about €4,618 per month when spread over twelve months. [ISA 2026–27 fees](https://www.isa.nl/fees/).\n\nConfirm admissions eligibility, places, transport, meals and registration charges. International schooling is excluded from every standard family estimate in this guide.\n\n### University tuition and student support\n\nThe ordinary statutory higher-education tuition fee is **€2,694 for academic year 2026–27**, equivalent to €224.50 monthly over twelve months. Eligibility depends on nationality or residence status, the institution and programme, and prior Dutch degrees. EU/EEA status alone is not a complete tuition assessment. Students outside the statutory-fee rules pay an institution- and programme-specific fee, which can be substantially higher. [DUO tuition fees](https://www.duo.nl/particulier/tuition-fees.jsp), [Erasmus University’s 2026–27 tuition explanation](https://www.eur.nl/en/education/practical-matters/registration/tuition-fee/tuition-fee-2026-2027).\n\nDo not divide tuition by twelve and assume that is the institution’s payment schedule. Application charges, books, equipment and permit-related financial requirements may need cash earlier.\n\nDUO support and the student travel product have their own conditions. A loan provides financing but remains debt; it does not make the underlying living cost disappear. [DUO eligibility](https://www.duo.nl/particulier/student-finance/eligibility.jsp).\n\n### Child benefit\n\nFrom the third quarter of 2026, SVB’s quarterly child-benefit amounts are **€298.40 for ages 0–5, €362.35 for ages 6–11 and €426.29 for ages 12–17**. The Q3 payment date is 1 October 2026. These are quarterly amounts, not monthly payments. Eligibility and cross-border coordination must be checked. [SVB benefit amounts and payment dates](https://www.svb.nl/en/child-benefit/amounts-and-payment-dates/child-benefit-amounts).\n\nTwo eligible children aged six and nine would therefore correspond to €724.70 per quarter, or about €241.57 monthly for budgeting. This possible income is not deducted from the family’s gross spending total below. Other support, including the income-dependent child budget, requires a separate entitlement calculation.\n\n## Salaries and Purchasing Power\n\nThe latest full-year earnings observations are historical statistics, even when published in 2026.\n\n| Measure | Verified reference | Interpretation |\n| --- | --- | --- |\n| Statutory adult minimum wage from 1 July 2026 | €14.99 gross/hour, age 21+ | Lower youth rates apply |\n| CBS average annual wage, 2025 | €40,800 | All-employee measure; not a universal full-time salary |\n| CBS average special remuneration, 2025 | €6,600 | Reported separately from the annual wage above |\n| CBS median hourly wage in the cited occupation table, 2024 | €25.30 | Employee main-job measure; not a monthly median salary |\n\nThe average annual wage and special-remuneration amounts come from the [CBS annual-wage dashboard](https://www.cbs.nl/nl-nl/visualisaties/dashboard-arbeidsmarkt/ontwikkeling-cao-lonen/jaarloon-werknemers-per-bedrijfstak). The median is from the 2024 occupation table in [CBS’s labour-market publication](https://longreads.cbs.nl/dearbeidsmarktincijfers-2025/lonen-en-loonkosten/). The populations and definitions differ; the median must not be directly compared with a full-time annual package without explaining hours and included remuneration.\n\nSince the minimum is hourly, there is no single fixed statutory monthly minimum. At €14.99, an annualised 40-hour week corresponds to approximately €2,598 gross per month using 52 weeks divided by twelve; 36 hours corresponds to €2,338. These are arithmetic equivalents using the July rate throughout, not actual average pay for calendar year 2026 or legal monthly floor amounts. [Government minimum-wage rates](https://www.government.nl/themes/work/minimum-wage/minimum-wage-amounts).\n\n### A transparent net-pay example\n\nAssume €48,000 annual taxable employment income, including holiday allowance; a full-year Dutch resident below state-pension age; no employee pension deduction, other income, special deductions or expatriate relief.\n\nApplying the 2026 Box 1 rates and general and employment tax credits gives approximately:\n\n| Calculation | Annual amount |\n| --- | --- |\n| Taxable gross employment income | €48,000 |\n| Tax and national insurance before credits | €17,325 |\n| General tax credit | €1,946 |\n| Employment tax credit | €5,528 |\n| Net income after these items | **€38,150** |\n| Annual net divided by twelve | **About €3,179/month** |\n\nThis is an illustrative annual tax calculation, with amounts rounded for display. It is not a forecast of every monthly payslip. Separately paid holiday allowance changes payment timing; pension contributions, benefits in kind and individual circumstances change the result. The personal health-insurance premium is still payable from this net income and appears in the expense budgets.\n\nThe calculation uses the official [2026 income-tax brackets](https://www.belastingdienst.nl/wps/wcm/connect/nl/werk-en-inkomen/content/hoeveel-inkomstenbelasting-betalen), [general tax-credit rules](https://www.belastingdienst.nl/wps/wcm/connect/fisin/fisin2026/heffingskortingen) and [2026 employment-credit table](https://www.belastingdienst.nl/wps/wcm/connect/bldcontentnl/belastingdienst/prive/inkomstenbelasting/heffingskortingen_boxen_tarieven/heffingskortingen/arbeidskorting/tabel-arbeidskorting-2026).\n\nCompare offers by contracted hours, pension deductions, holiday allowance, bonuses and confirmed commuting support. This guide does not invent a current average salary for each city. Job mix and hours differ, so a regional household-income statistic would not establish what an individual employee should earn.\n\nFor more detail, use [Average Salary in the Netherlands](https://finorum.com/average-salary-in-the-netherlands/) and the [Finorum Netherlands Net Salary Calculator](https://finorum.com/netherlands-net-salary-calculator/). Tax residence, social-insurance status, family circumstances and deductions affect disposable income.\n\n## Monthly Budgets by Household Type\n\nThese are internally consistent **illustrations**, not official minimum-income standards. All amounts are monthly euros and include annualised provisions where stated. Benefits and employer reimbursements are excluded unless explicitly discussed.\n\nBasic rent excludes the separately listed bills. Service charges in these examples do not include energy or drinking water. Health reserves are money set aside, not a claim that every household spends that amount on treatment every month.\n\n### Single Person\n\nOne adult renting a modest one-bedroom apartment in Rotterdam or The Hague, with local travel and cycling, no car and no expensive intercity commute.\n\n| Item | Monthly amount |\n| --- | --- |\n| Basic rent | €1,650 |\n| Service charges | €60 |\n| Electricity and heating | €170 |\n| Drinking water | €20 |\n| Municipal and water-board tax provision | €65 |\n| Internet and one mobile plan | €60 |\n| Groceries | €350 |\n| Local transport and bicycle upkeep | €110 |\n| Basic health-insurance premium | €160 |\n| Deductible and other health reserve | €50 |\n| Eating out, leisure and fitness | €160 |\n| Contents/liability cover, toiletries, clothing and replacements | €100 |\n| **Total including rent** | **€2,955** |\n| **Total excluding basic rent** | **€1,305** |\n\nThe last €100 line includes a €15 household-insurance allowance. At the illustrative €3,179 net income above, the remaining margin is only about €224; more expensive rent or commuting would quickly absorb it.\n\nKeeping everything else unchanged and paying €2,100 Amsterdam rent would raise this example to **€3,405**. That is a rent sensitivity calculation, not a separate claim about the average Amsterdam resident.\n\n### Couple\n\nTwo adults sharing a two-bedroom apartment outside the centre of a major city. Both use cycling and local public transport; there is no car.\n\n| Item | Monthly amount |\n| --- | --- |\n| Basic rent | €1,850 |\n| Service charges | €70 |\n| Electricity and heating | €210 |\n| Drinking water | €25 |\n| Municipal and water-board tax provision | €80 |\n| Internet and two mobile plans | €75 |\n| Groceries | €600 |\n| Local transport and bicycle upkeep | €200 |\n| Two basic health-insurance premiums | €320 |\n| Health reserve for two adults | €100 |\n| Eating out, leisure and fitness | €250 |\n| Household insurance, toiletries, clothing and replacements | €170 |\n| **Household total** | **€3,950** |\n\nThe final line includes €20 for household insurance. Sharing accommodation, broadband and heating means the couple’s total is not twice the single-person budget. Dividing the total into equal personal [shares](https://finorum.com/what-is-a-stock/) is a household choice, not a legal allocation of bills.\n\n### Family With Two Children\n\nTwo adults and children aged **six and nine**, renting a modest three-bedroom home outside the centre of Rotterdam, The Hague or a comparable city. The children attend ordinary publicly funded school. Parents arrange care themselves; **paid BSO and daycare are excluded**.\n\n| Item | Monthly amount |\n| --- | --- |\n| Basic rent | €2,300 |\n| Service charges | €90 |\n| Electricity and heating | €280 |\n| Drinking water | €35 |\n| Municipal and water-board tax provision | €85 |\n| Internet and mobile plans | €85 |\n| Groceries | €900 |\n| Local transport and bicycle upkeep | €260 |\n| Two adult basic health-insurance premiums | €320 |\n| Household health reserve | €120 |\n| School incidentals and children’s activities | €150 |\n| Eating out, family leisure and fitness | €280 |\n| Household insurance, toiletries, clothing and replacements | €250 |\n| **Household total before benefits** | **€5,155** |\n\nHousehold insurance accounts for €25 within the final line. The health reserve includes adult deductible provisions and other modest out-of-pocket costs. It does not fund substantial orthodontics, private therapy or long-term care.\n\nIf eligible for the child benefit described above, the household could apply approximately €241.57 monthly equivalent toward these costs, leaving **about €4,913.43 to fund from other income**. Actual benefit arrives quarterly. No housing, healthcare or childcare benefit has been assumed.\n\nIf the parents need paid BSO, add the actual invoice less confirmed childcare benefit. If they need a car, add a vehicle-specific ownership budget and remove only the public-transport spending genuinely replaced. Those adjustments can materially change the family’s income target.\n\n### Student\n\nA study-only international student with a room in a shared home in a regional university city, within cycling distance of classes. This model assumes no Dutch basic-insurance obligation and no free DUO travel product.\n\n| Item | Monthly amount |\n| --- | --- |\n| Room basic rent | €650 |\n| Shared energy, water, service charges and internet | €100 |\n| Mobile plan | €15 |\n| Share of local taxes | €20 |\n| Groceries | €250 |\n| Local travel and bicycle upkeep | €50 |\n| Suitable student/home-country insurance allowance | €70 |\n| Study materials and equipment provision | €70 |\n| Leisure | €65 |\n| Toiletries, clothing and personal/contents cover | €40 |\n| **Living costs excluding tuition** | **€1,330** |\n| Statutory tuition provision, if eligible: €2,694 ÷ 12 | €224.50 |\n| **Living costs plus statutory tuition provision** | **€1,554.50** |\n\nThe insurance line is a planning allowance, not a universal student-policy premium. Verify coverage and any ongoing home-country contribution. If paid employment makes Dutch basic insurance mandatory, replace the €70 with an appropriate basic premium and health reserve; do not simply keep both without a reason.\n\nInstitutional tuition replaces the €224.50 line when applicable. A €900 room would add €250 to this model. For an all-inclusive room, allocate its included bills within the €750 housing-and-shared-bills total instead of adding them again. Municipal tax remission or shared-house assessment rules can also alter the €20 provision.\n\n## Cost Differences Between Cities\n\nThis comparison uses a **single adult renting a modest non-central independent apartment**, September 2026 planning allowances, no car and mostly home-prepared food. Transport amounts are personal budgets, not named pass prices.\n\n| City or area | Basic studio / one-bedroom rent | Local transport and cycling | Groceries | Overall tendency | Important local consideration |\n| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |\n| Amsterdam | €1,450–2,200 | €70–150 | €300–450 | Highest housing pressure in this comparison | Check the precise connection from an outer neighbourhood and any airport commute |\n| Rotterdam | €1,150–1,700 | €70–150 | €300–450 | Often below Amsterdam | A job in the wider port area may require more than a simple urban journey |\n| The Hague | €1,100–1,700 | €70–150 | €300–450 | Similar broad range to Rotterdam | International-school location can dictate neighbourhood and transport |\n| Utrecht | €1,200–1,800 | €60–140 | €300–450 | Expensive relative to its size | A convenient rail location does not make a peak-hour commute inexpensive |\n| Eindhoven | €1,050–1,600 | €60–140 | €290–430 | Potential housing saving | Price travel to the actual workplace, not only to the city centre |\n| Enschede or nearby regional towns | €800–1,200 | €50–130 | €280–420 | Lower housing allowance | Confirm suitable vacancies and whether the household can remain car-free |\n\nGrocery differences are deliberately small: chains sell similar products across regions. The clearest potential saving is usually housing. These ranges should not be used to infer that every Enschede supermarket is cheaper than every Amsterdam one.\n\nA €350 rent saving accompanied by €250 more travel spending produces only a €100 cash saving before valuing the extra travel time. Compare the job, home and commute together. For remote workers, check whether the dwelling has enough space for work and whether the employment arrangement permits working from the Netherlands.\n\n## How Much Income Is Needed?\n\nThe targets below apply a **10–20% margin above the article’s worked spending budgets**. They are household net-income planning targets, not official subsistence thresholds, lender criteria or gross salary requirements.\n\n| Household | Worked monthly spending | Net income with a 10–20% margin |\n| --- | --- | --- |\n| Single renter in the major-city example | €2,955 | About €3,250–3,550 |\n| Couple, combined income | €3,950 | About €4,345–4,740 |\n| Family with two school-age children, before benefits | €5,155 | About €5,670–6,190 |\n\nThe margin is additional to routine replacement and health reserves already included. It allows some room for saving and unexpected costs, but does not fund a house deposit, major debt repayments or private education automatically.\n\nFor the single-person Amsterdam rent sensitivity of €3,405, the same method gives roughly **€3,750–4,090 net monthly**. Room-sharing or a secure regulated tenancy can reduce the required amount substantially.\n\nLandlords may still reject an application that meets these net-spending targets if it fails their gross-income screening. Likewise, a high gross salary does not guarantee that enough remains after pension deductions, tax and other commitments.\n\nUse the [Netherlands Net Salary Calculator](https://finorum.com/netherlands-net-salary-calculator/) and [EU Cost of Living Comparison](https://finorum.com/cost-of-living-comparison/) with the correct household and pay assumptions. A couple’s target is combined net income; it is not a salary that each partner must earn.\n\n## Cost-Saving Strategies\n\nStart with costs that repeat every month:\n\nHousing benefit changed materially in **2026**: a rent above the previous eligibility ceiling no longer automatically prevents a claim. Income, assets, household and dwelling conditions still apply. For adults aged 21 and over, benefit is calculated only up to the relevant maximum rent of €932.93; service charges no longer enter the calculation. Younger households have different limits and exceptions. This does not mean the state pays the excess on an expensive apartment. [Government 2026 housing-benefit explanation](https://www.rijksoverheid.nl/actueel/nieuws/2025/11/24/meer-mensen-in-aanmerking-huurtoeslag).\n\nBudget without unconfirmed support when signing a lease. Once an entitlement is established, enter it as income or a clearly identified reduction in the amount you must fund, consistently throughout the budget.\n\n## Relocating to the Netherlands\n\nThe first month can require much more cash than the ongoing budget. Separate money you spend permanently from refundable deposits, ordinary first-month bills and money kept as a reserve.\n\n| Arrival item | One adult | Couple or family | Treatment |\n| --- | --- | --- | --- |\n| Deposit | €1,300–4,400 | €1,800–6,000 | Refundable security; based on the lease and legal limit |\n| First month’s basic rent | €1,300–2,200 | €1,800–3,000 | Ordinary recurring expense paid upfront |\n| Moving, short temporary stay, basic furnishing and setup | €1,500–4,000 | €3,000–7,000 | One-time estimate; origin and furniture condition matter |\n| Accessible emergency reserve | €3,000–6,000 | €5,000–10,000 | Savings retained, not a fee |\n| **Indicative arrival cash envelope** | **€7,100–16,600** | **€11,600–26,000** | Sum of the four distinct categories |\n\nThe envelope includes the first month’s basic rent once. Other first-month living costs still come from the household’s ordinary operating budget. Do not add a second full rent charge when combining the tables.\n\nThe permanent setup-spending allowance is €1,500–4,000 for one adult or €3,000–7,000 for a couple or family. It can cover a short temporary stay, transport of belongings, modest furniture, a used bicycle and practical connection costs. A prolonged hotel search, overseas container move, empty-shell property or several new beds and appliances can exceed it substantially.\n\nResidence-permit fees, certified translations, legalisation and any required application service are **additional where applicable**; the cost depends on the route and number of applicants. Check the relevant [IND residence-permit and application information](https://ind.nl/en). University tuition, school registration and a car purchase are also outside this arrival envelope.\n\nIf you expect to live in the Netherlands for more than four months, register as a resident with the municipality’s BRP within five days of arrival. This leads to a BSN. Arrange the appointment and required documents promptly, and ensure the accommodation supports proper registration. [Government BRP registration guidance](https://www.government.nl/faq/when-should-i-register-with-the-personal-records-database-as-a-resident).\n\nAt handover, photograph the property and meters, check keys and inventory, and establish whose name is on each utility contract. Arrange health coverage according to your actual status rather than waiting for the first payslip.\n\nRemote employees should confirm payroll, residence and social-insurance arrangements before assuming that a foreign employment contract settles those questions. Self-employed workers need a separate business-cost and unpaid-leave reserve. Retirees should verify pension taxation and health-cover coordination; the employee net-pay illustration is not a pension calculation.\n\nUse the [European Relocation Score Calculator](https://finorum.com/european-relocation-calculator/) to compare locations, then replace general estimates with written housing, moving and insurance quotations.\n\n## Is the Netherlands Expensive Compared With the Rest of Europe?\n\nThe official comparative picture is uneven. The following **2025 price-level indices use EU = 100**:\n\n| Household-consumption category | Netherlands index |\n| --- | --- |\n| Overall household consumption | 115.6 |\n| Food and non-alcoholic beverages | 99.4 |\n| Housing, water, electricity, gas and other fuels | 134.2 |\n| Transport | 112.2 |\n| Restaurants and accommodation | 119.9 |\n| Clothing and footwear | 92.8 |\n\nThese figures come from the [Eurostat-based comparative tables published by Statistics Norway](https://www.ssb.no/en/priser-og-prisindekser/konsumpriser/statistikk/sammenlikning-av-prisniva-i-europa). The housing category is broader than a newcomer’s rent, and the restaurant category also includes accommodation.\n\n**Actual individual consumption per person was 119% of the EU average in volume terms; its price-level index was 120.4.** Volume and price are different measures. The volume index includes individually consumed public services. It does not mean a €1,000 basket costs €1,190. [Comparative consumption and price tables](https://www.ssb.no/en/priser-og-prisindekser/konsumpriser/statistikk/sammenlikning-av-prisniva-i-europa).\n\nFor a mover, the relevant comparison is the actual housing and lifestyle affordable on the available net income. A national food index close to the EU average does not offset a large rental premium automatically. Equally, high national prices do not mean every established resident faces the same affordability problem.\n\nUse the [EU Cost of Living Comparison](https://finorum.com/cost-of-living-comparison/) to explore household scenarios, keeping the difference between price indices and personal budgets in mind.\n\n## Related Taxes and Investment Considerations\n\nFor everyday affordability, the principal tax-related items are wage taxation, local household charges, vehicle tax where relevant and the interaction between income and benefits. Mandatory personal health-insurance premiums are paid separately from the net-pay example.\n\nOwner-occupiers have costs that tenants do not, including ownership-related municipal and water-board charges, maintenance and potentially financing costs. Investment income, foreign assets or a rental property can also affect tax and means-tested support. Do not apply a simple capital-gains percentage to every Dutch investment situation or assume an expatriate concession applies automatically.\n\nFor detailed treatment, consult [Investing in the Netherlands](https://finorum.com/investing-in-the-netherlands/), the [Netherlands Tax Guide](https://finorum.com/netherlands-tax-guide/), the [Netherlands Capital Gains Tax Calculator](https://finorum.com/netherlands-capital-gains-tax-calculator/) and [Rental Property Tax Calculator Europe](https://finorum.com/rental-income-tax-calculator-europe/). These are supporting resources, not substitutes for a household-specific tax assessment.\n\n## Related Finorum Guides and Calculators\n\n## Disclaimer\n\nPrices and budgets are indicative and can change with location, provider, season, availability, consumption and household circumstances. Official statistics refer to their stated periods; planning ranges and worked budgets are editorial estimates, not guaranteed prices, official minimum-income standards or entitlement decisions.\n\nVerify current contracts, tariffs, benefit eligibility and legal requirements with the relevant authority or provider before committing. This guide is for general information and does not constitute financial, tax, legal or relocation advice.\n\n##### [Iva Buće](https://finorum.com/author/ajvifinorum/)\n\nIva Buće is a Master of Economics specializing in digital marketing and logistics. She combines analytical thinking with creativity to make financial and investment topics accessible to a broader audience. At Finorum, she focuses on translating complex economic concepts into clear, practical insights for everyday readers and investors.\n\n### Sources & References\n\n#### EU regulations & taxation\n\n#### Additional educational resources\n\nCost of Living in Sweden 2026: Complete Breakdown\n\nNet Salary Calculator Europe\n\n## Related Posts\n\n### [Investing in the Netherlands: Complete Guide for 2026](https://finorum.com/investing-in-the-netherlands/)\n\n### [Tax Guide Netherlands 2026: What Every Investor Needs to Know](https://finorum.com/netherlands-tax-guide/)\n\n### [Average Salary in the Netherlands 2026: Gross, Net and What You Actually Keep](https://finorum.com/average-salary-in-the-netherlands/)\n\n![](https://finorum.com/wp-content/uploads/2025/10/LOGO-1.png)\n\nCopyright © 2026\n\n![trading.com](https://finorum.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/07/Campaign-logo-black-transparent-1.png)\n\nTo provide the best experiences, we use technologies like cookies to store and/or access device information. Consenting to these technologies will allow us to process data such as browsing behavior or unique IDs on this site. Not consenting or withdrawing consent may adversely affect certain features and functions. For more information, see our [Cookie Policy].\n\nTable of Contents",
