Prices may differ in your area\n\n#### Hygienic and household products\n\nHygienic and household products include everything from toothpaste and shampoo to dish soap (or washing-up liquid) and toilet bleach. [...] Compared to other capital cities, Amsterdam’s cost of living is (as of January 2026):\n\n 31% cheaper than New York (the US)\n 12% cheaper than London (the UK)\n 30% more expensive than Berlin (Germany)\n 71.% more expensive than Warsaw (Poland)\n 147% more expensive than Ankara (Türkiye)\n 347% more expensive than Delhi (India)\n\n### The cost of living in Rotterdam\n\nAs the second largest city in the Netherlands, Rotterdam is an urban hotspot that attracts increasingly more expats. After sustaining catastrophic damage during World War II (WW2), the city literally rose from the ashes and rebuilt itself as one of the country’s most architecturally innovative and experimental places.\n\nMan holding his young child and paying an oliebollenkraam using his phone.\n\nPhoto: Lorado/Getty Images [...] Alcohol is a lot cheaper in supermarkets. You can get a beer from €1–3, depending on the make and size. A decent bottle of wine usually costs around €5–9. The price of most liquor (e.g., gin, vodka, or jenever) starts from €10–11, depending on your drink of choice.\n\nWoman looking at the alcohol shelf in an Albert Heijn supermarket.\n\nAlbert Heijn (Photo: Ifeelstock/Dreamstime)",
      "score": 0.3223238,
      "raw_content": "![Country Flag](https://admin.expatica.com/nl/wp-content/uploads/sites/3/2023/10/nl.svg)![](data:image/svg+xml,%3c?xml%20version='1.0'%20encoding='UTF-8'?%3e%3csvg%20width='13px'%20height='8px'%20viewBox='0%200%2013%208'%20version='1.1'%20xmlns='http://www.w3.org/2000/svg'%20xmlns:xlink='http://www.w3.org/1999/xlink'%3e%3ctitle%3eico-down%3c/title%3e%3cg%20id='assets'%20stroke='none'%20stroke-width='1'%20fill='none'%20fill-rule='evenodd'%3e%3cg%20transform='translate(-1091.000000,%20-254.000000)'%20id='ico-down'%20stroke='%23000000'%20stroke-width='1.5'%3e%3cg%20transform='translate(1092.250000,%20254.750000)'%3e%3cpolyline%20id='Path-2-Copy-4'%20transform='translate(5.500000,%202.750000)%20scale(1,%20-1)%20rotate(-180.000000)%20translate(-5.500000,%20-2.750000)%20'%20points='0%200%205.5%205.5%2011%200'%3e%3c/polyline%3e%3c/g%3e%3c/g%3e%3c/g%3e%3c/svg%3e)\n\n[![Expatica logo](/_nuxt/logo-by-wise.XDKAXrBJ.svg)](/)\n\n* [Canada (Français)](/fr-ca/)\n* [Estados Unidos (Português)](/pt-us/)\n\n\n\n\n\n* [Netherlands](/nl/)\n* [Portugal (Português)](/pt-pt/)\n\n* [Saudi Arabia](/sa/)\n* [South Africa](/za/)\n\n\n* [United Arab Emirates](/ae/)\n* [United Kingdom](/uk/)\n\n[Home](/nl/)[Education](/nl/education/) [Finance](/nl/finance/) [Healthcare](/nl/healthcare/) [Housing](/nl/housing/) [Lifestyle](/nl/lifestyle/)  [Living](/nl/living/) [Moving](/nl/moving/) [Working](/nl/working/)[Directory](/nl/directory/)\n\n[Disclaimers](#)\n\n* [Is the Netherlands’ economy good?](#overview)\n* [The cost of living in the Netherlands in 2026](#living-costs)\n* [How does the Netherlands compare to the rest of the world?](#comparison)\n* [Financial support for the cost of living in the Netherlands](#financial-aid)\n* [Tips on how to save money in the Netherlands](#tips)\n* [Useful resources](#resources)\n\n[Disclaimers](#)\n\nAbout the Netherlands\n\n# The cost of living in the Netherlands in 2026\n\nLooking for work in Amsterdam? Before you pack your bags, discover how affordable the cost of living in the Netherlands in 2026 really is.\n\n![Laura van der Most](https://admin.expatica.com/nl/wp-content/uploads/2023/11/laura-vandermost-150x150.jpg)\n\nWritten by\n\n![](https://admin.expatica.com/nl/wp-content/uploads/2023/11/laura-vandermost-150x150.jpg)\n\nLaura van der Most\n\nOriginally from the Netherlands, Laura speaks Dutch, English, un peu French, and sehr schlechtes German.\n\n[Full profile →](https://www.expatica.com/authors/laura-van-der-most/)\n\nLast updated\n\n![People shopping at an outdoor market in Assen, the Netherlands.](https://admin.expatica.com/nl/wp-content/uploads/sites/3/2017/05/cost-of-living-in-netherlands-1536x1024.jpg)\n\nWith a thriving economy and one of the highest minimum wages in Europe, the Netherlands is a great place to bring home the literal and figurative cheese. But how does the country’s living expenses stack up? From everyday costs like groceries and utilities to housing and healthcare, here’s a clear picture of the cost of living in the Netherlands and help you prepare for your new adventure.\n\nAs you plan your move, managing your finances across currencies is an important consideration. Whether you’re receiving your salary, paying rent, or transferring savings to the Netherlands, understanding how currency exchange works can help you budget more effectively. Services like **[Wise](https://wise.com/nl/account/?utm_source=expatica-com&utm_medium=affiliate&utm_campaign=guides&utm_content=nl/moving/about/cost-of-living-in-netherlands-1085103/)** use the mid-market exchange rate with transparent fees, which could help reduce the cost of international transfers and currency conversions.\n\nContinue reading for the following information:\n\n### Wise\n\nIf you’re moving money abroad, check out Wise. This well-known platform provides competitive rates, low fees, and transparent transactions. Experience efficient, cost-effective international transfers by joining Wise today.\n\n[Go to website\n\n![](https://admin.expatica.com/wp-content/uploads/2023/11/ico-external-link.svg)](https://wise.com/nl/account/?utm_source=expatica-com&utm_medium=affiliate&utm_campaign=guides&utm_content=nl/moving/about/cost-of-living-in-netherlands-1085103/)\n\n## Is the Netherlands’ economy good?\n\nWhile a tiny country, the Netherlands packs a powerful punch. With a gross domestic product (GDP) of [€1.122 billion](https://ec.europa.eu/eurostat/databrowser/view/nama_10_gdp/default/table?lang=en) (2024), it is the fifth-largest economy in the European Union (EU), trailing behind Germany, France, Italy, and Spain.\n\n### Total GDP\n\n€1.122 billion\n\n### GDP per person\n\n€57,800\n\n### GDP growth 2025\n\n1.5% (projected)\n\nThe GDP works out to [€63,000 per capita](https://www.cbs.nl/en-gb/news/2025/19/gdp-per-capita-netherlands-fourth-in-eu-in-2024), which the [European Commission (EC)](https://economy-finance.ec.europa.eu/economic-surveillance-eu-economies/netherlands/economic-forecast-netherlands_en) forecasts will grow by 1.3% in 2026 and 1.7% in 2027. This growth is on par with the [EU average of 1.4% and 1.5%,](https://economy-finance.ec.europa.eu/economic-forecast-and-surveys/economic-forecasts/autumn-2025-economic-forecast-shows-continued-growth-despite-challenging-environment_en) respectively.\n\n![Typical Amsterdam cityscape with canals, boats, and houses.](https://admin.expatica.com/nl/wp-content/uploads/sites/3/2024/09/amsterdam-canals.jpg)\n\nAmsterdam, the Netherlands (Photo: Adrien Olichon/Pexels)\n\n### Inflation and disposable income in the Netherlands\n\nUnfortunately for the Dutch, the GDP per capita doesn’t mean all residents have just under 58K to spend. The equivalized disposable income (i.e., the total net income of all household members divided by the weighted number of household members) is [€31,981 per person per year.](https://ec.europa.eu/eurostat/databrowser/view/ILC_DI03__custom_6622519/bookmark/table?lang=en&bookmarkId=eef1da5d-2079-4b0a-865c-338b87134baf) This is well above the EU average of €21,582 per person (2024).\n\n### Disposable income\n\n€27,091 per person\n\n### Risk of poverty\n\n11.7% of the population\n\n### 2025 inflation\n\n3.0% (projected)\n\nAround [15% of the Dutch population](https://ec.europa.eu/eurostat/statistics-explained/index.php?title=Living_conditions_in_Europe_-_poverty_and_social_exclusion) is at risk of poverty or social exclusion, compared to an EU average of 21% (2024). This makes the Netherlands the third best-performing country in the EU. According to the [Central Bureau of Statistics](https://www.cbs.nl/en-gb/news/2025/51/rise-in-poverty-rate-after-five-years-of-decreases) (*centraal bureau van statistiek* – cbs), 3.1% of the population were in actual poverty in 2024.\n\nThe low poverty line is likely explained due to the Netherlands’ high minimum wage standards and social security system. You can read more about the average income in our article on [minimum wage in the Netherlands](https://www.expatica.com/nl/working/employment-law/minimum-wage-in-the-netherlands-852578/).\n\nIn terms of purchasing power, the Netherlands’ [average headline inflation](https://economy-finance.ec.europa.eu/economic-surveillance-eu-economies/netherlands/economic-forecast-netherlands_en) for 2026 and 2027 is projected to be 2.5% and 2.1%, respectively. This is close the [EU average of 2.1% and 2.2%.](https://economy-finance.ec.europa.eu/economic-forecast-and-surveys/economic-forecasts/autumn-2025-economic-forecast-shows-continued-growth-despite-challenging-environment_en)\n\n![#](https://admin.expatica.com/nl/wp-content/uploads/sites/3/2023/11/minimum-wage-netherlands.jpeg)\n\n[Working\n\nMinimum wage and average salary in the Netherlands\n\n[Read more](https://www.expatica.com/nl/working/employment-law/minimum-wage-in-the-netherlands-852578/)](https://www.expatica.com/nl/working/employment-law/minimum-wage-in-the-netherlands-852578/)\n\n## The cost of living in the Netherlands in 2026\n\nUnfortunately, the [most recent data](https://longreads.cbs.nl/the-netherlands-in-numbers-2022/what-do-we-spend-money-on/) for Dutch household expenses is from 2020. This article will therefore update these figures to account for inflation between 2020-2025 and the predicted inflation for 2026 (approximately 30% increase in total).\n\n![Man eating breakfast and drinking orange juice while he is making a budget on a laptop at the kitchen table.](https://admin.expatica.com/nl/wp-content/uploads/sites/3/2024/09/kitchen-table-laptop-orange-juice.jpg)\n\nPhoto: Maskot/Getty Images\n\nBreaking down the costs:\n\n|  |  |\n| --- | --- |\n| **Annually** | **Monthly** |\n| Food and non-alcoholic beverages | €5,733 | €478 |\n| Housing | €15,106 | €1,258 |\n| Clothing and footwear | €1,775 | €148 |\n| Household | €2,958 | €246 |\n| Hospitality and restaurants | €2,139 | €178 |\n| Communication | €1,593 | €133 |\n| Culture and recreation | €3,868 | €322 |\n| Alcohol | €1,547 | €129 |\n| Transport | €5,096 | €425 |\n| Other | €5,688 | €474 |\n\nEstimated cost of living in 2026\n\nAlongside your monthly living costs, allow for the one-off expense of moving your belongings to the Netherlands. For a move from the UK, a quote from a provider such as [Kanguro](https://kanguro.co.uk/removals-from-england-to-netherlands/), which offers shared-load and dedicated removals, can help you compare the cost of transporting furniture with replacing it after arrival.\n\nKeep reading for an overview of the cost of living expenses you might come across in your daily life.\n\n### Grocery costs in the Netherlands in 2026\n\n#### Beer, wine, and liquor\n\nIf there is a Dutch word everyone around the world knows, it’s Heineken. And apartheid. Also gratis. But the point is that the Netherlands is a big name in alcoholic beverages. In 2023, the country was the [fourth-largest beer producer in the EU,](https://ec.europa.eu/eurostat/web/products-eurostat-news/w/edn-20240802-1) after Germany, Spain, and Poland. Dutch drinkers consume around [7.7 liters of pure alcohol](https://www.who.int/data/gho/data/indicators/indicator-details/GHO/alcohol-recorded-per-capita-(15-)-consumption-(in-litres-of-pure-alcohol)) per year (2020), with most people preferring [beer](https://www.expatica.com/nl/lifestyle/food-drink/dutch-beer-100786/).\n\nA pilsner (50cl) in a local bar or restaurant will set you back [€4–8](https://www.numbeo.com/cost-of-living/country_result.jsp?country=Netherlands), while a craft beer (33cl) costs between €47.25. A glass of wine, on the other hand, costs between €4.50–7.00.\n\nAlcohol is a lot cheaper in supermarkets. You can get a beer from €1–3, depending on the make and size. A decent bottle of wine usually costs around €5–9. The price of most liquor (e.g., gin, vodka, or jenever) starts from €10–11, depending on your drink of choice.\n\n![Woman looking at the alcohol shelf in an Albert Heijn supermarket.](https://admin.expatica.com/nl/wp-content/uploads/sites/3/2023/11/albert-heijn-supermarket.jpg)\n\nAlbert Heijn (Photo: Ifeelstock/Dreamstime)\n\nWhen shopping for groceries and daily essentials, the payment method you use can affect your overall costs. If you’re using a card from your home country, your bank may charge foreign transaction fees (typically 2–3%) plus apply an exchange rate markup on every purchase. The **[Wise card](https://wise.com/nl/card/?utm_source=expatica-com&utm_medium=affiliate&utm_campaign=guides&utm_content=nl/moving/about/cost-of-living-in-netherlands-1085103/)** uses the mid-market exchange rate for currency conversion, which could help you avoid these extra costs on everyday purchases. You can hold money in 40+ currencies in your [Wise Account](https://wise.com/nl/account/?utm_source=expatica-com&utm_medium=affiliate&utm_campaign=guides&utm_content=nl/moving/about/cost-of-living-in-netherlands-1085103/), including euros to make purchases conveniently in the Netherlands.\n\n#### Food and drink\n\nGroceries are the second-largest expense after housing costs. In 2026, households in the Netherlands will spend an average of €478 per month on food and non-alcoholic drinks.\n\nThe average price ranges\\* of basic [supermarket](https://www.expatica.com/nl/living/household/dutch-supermarkets-and-grocery-shopping-106435/) items include:\n\n|  |  |  |  |\n| --- | --- | --- | --- |\n| **Product** | **Price range** | **Product** | **Price range** |\n| **Apples (1 kilo)** | €1.60–3.20 | **Potatoes (1 kilo)** | €1.00–2.00 |\n| **Bread (1 loaf)** | €1.00–3.00 | **Regular milk (1 liter)** | €0.95–1.50 |\n| **Cheese (1 kilo)** | €8.50–20.00 | **Rice (1 kilo)** | €1.50–3.25 |\n| **Coffee (1 kilo)** | €10.00–25.00 | **Tea (per bag)** | €0.15–0.18 |\n| **Eggs (12)** | €2.50–5.00 | **Tomatoes (1 kilo)** | €2.00–6.00 |\n| **Orange juice (1 liter)** | €1.00–3.75 | **Water (1.5 liter)** | €0.50–1.50 |\n\n\\*Based on [Numbeo](https://www.numbeo.com/cost-of-living/country_result.jsp?country=Netherlands) (15th January 2026). Prices may differ in your area\n\n#### Hygienic and household products\n\nHygienic and household products include everything from toothpaste and shampoo to dish soap (or washing-up liquid) and toilet bleach.\n\nThe average price ranges\\* of these necessities include:\n\n|  |  |  |  |\n| --- | --- | --- | --- |\n| **Product** | **Price range** | **Product** | **Price range** |\n| **All-purpose cleaner (1 liter)** | €0.99–3.79 | **Shampoo (1 liter)** | €2.00–22.03 |\n| **Deodorant (150 ml)** | €2.74–7.99 | **Soap (1 liter)** | €2.64–25.91 |\n| **Dish soap (1 liter)** | €1.55–10.00 | **Tampons (per tampon)** | €0.05–0.32 |\n| **Garbage bags (per bag)** | €0.08–0.18 | **Toilet paper (per roll)** | €0.35–0.98 |\n| **Laundry detergent (per wash)** | €0.12–0.65 | **Toothpaste (75 ml)** | €1.59–10.80 |\n\n\\*Prices may differ in your area\n\n### 2026 housing costs in the Netherlands\n\nLike elsewhere, Dutch households spend most of their income on housing. The average housing costs in 2026 will be around €1,258 per month.\n\nAs in many other countries, rent and property prices in the Netherlands have soared in recent years, and finding affordable housing is a colossal challenge. Housing costs in the Netherlands were [33.8% higher than the EU average](https://ec.europa.eu/eurostat/web/interactive-publications/housing-2023#housing-cost) (2023).\n\nFor expats arranging rent payments or property deposits from abroad, it’s worth considering how you transfer money internationally. Large transactions like security deposits or down payments can incur significant costs when banks apply exchange rate markups on top of transfer fees.\n\n**[Wise](https://wise.com/nl/account/?utm_source=expatica-com&utm_medium=affiliate&utm_campaign=guides&utm_content=nl/moving/about/cost-of-living-in-netherlands-1085103/)** offers the mid-market exchange rate with transparent fees that could help you manage these expenses more effectively. Wise also has automatic fee discounts on large international transfers, helping you keep the costs low.\n\n[Learn more about Wise](https://wise.com/nl/account/?utm_source=expatica-com&utm_medium=affiliate&utm_campaign=guides&utm_content=nl/moving/about/cost-of-living-in-netherlands-1085103/)\n\n#### Rent and property prices\n\nOf course, housing costs vary significantly throughout the Netherlands. Studios and residential apartments in the *Randstad* (i.e., the country’s megalopolis) are comparatively more expensive than houses in rural and less popular areas. In fact, the Netherlands’ four largest cities – [Amsterdam](https://www.expatica.com/nl/moving/location/where-to-live-in-amsterdam-109117/), [Rotterdam](https://www.expatica.com/nl/moving/location/where-to-live-in-rotterdam-100625/), [The Hague](https://www.expatica.com/nl/moving/location/where-to-live-in-the-hague-103878/) (*Den Haag*), and [Utrecht](https://www.expatica.com/nl/moving/location/where-to-live-in-utrecht-100622/) – consistently rank among the [top 10 most highly-priced places in Europe.](https://housinganywhere.com/rent-index-by-city)\n\nIn 2026, the [average monthly rent](https://www.numbeo.com/cost-of-living/country_result.jsp?country=Netherlands) in the Netherlands is €1,157–1,444/month for a one-bedroom apartment and €1,800–2,337/month for a three-bedroom apartment. Potential homeowners will pay an average of [€537,661](https://www.hypotheker.nl/actueel/huizenprijzen-nederland/) (2025).\n\n### Kanguro\n\nKanguro provides reliable, affordable door-to-door removals between the UK and Europe. Our shared-load service means customers pay only for the vehicle space they use, making international moves cost-effective without compromising care. Vehicles operate in both directions six days a week, with UK-wide collection and customs support. Backed by 12 years of experience and more than 1,200 five-star Trustpilot reviews, Kanguro carefully manages boxes, furniture, flats and complete household moves.\n\n[Find out more\n\n![](https://admin.expatica.com/wp-content/uploads/2023/11/ico-external-link.svg)](https://kanguro.co.uk/removals-from-england-to-netherlands/)\n\nNo commitment required\n\nDepending on the location, you can expect the following average prices:\n\n|  |  |  |  |\n| --- | --- | --- | --- |\n| **Average monthly rent** for 1-bed in city center | **Average monthly rent for 3-bed in city center** | **Average property price** | **Average property price per square meter** (city center) |\n| **[Amsterdam](https://www.numbeo.com/cost-of-living/in/Amsterdam)** | €2,199 | €3,755 | €953,128 | €9,222 |\n| **[Groningen](https://www.numbeo.com/cost-of-living/in/Groningen)** | €1,140 | €1,743 | €361,000 | €4,732 |\n| **[Rotterdam](https://www.numbeo.com/cost-of-living/in/Rotterdam)** | €1,520 | €2,503 | €562,367 | €4,992 |\n| **[The Hague](https://www.numbeo.com/cost-of-living/in/The-Hague-Den-Haag-Netherlands)** | €1,504 | €2,719 | €811,444 | €5,427 |\n| **[Utrecht](https://www.numbeo.com/cost-of-living/in/Utrecht)** | €1,644 | €2,649 | €745,468 | €6,589 |\n\n\\*Numbeo (15th January 2026)\n\n![Construction of high rises in Rotterdam near a canal with barges.](https://admin.expatica.com/nl/wp-content/uploads/sites/3/2024/09/rotterdam-high-rises-canal.jpg)\n\nRotterdam, the Netherlands (Photo: Mike van den Berg/Unsplash)\n\nThe most expensive municipalities are Amsterdam, Laren, Bloemendaal, and Heemstede. However, if you’re looking to live in the Netherlands on a budget, the [cheapest places to move](https://www.hypotheker.nl/actueel/huizenprijzen-per-m2-per-gemeente/) to are:\n\n|  |  |  |  |  |\n| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |\n| **To rent** | **Average rent per square meter** | **To buy:** | **Average property price** | **Average property price per square meter** |\n| **Apeldoorn** | €14.72 | **Brunssum** | €244,000 | €2,160 |\n| **Helmond** | €13.10 | **Eemsdelta** | €249,500 | €2,119 |\n| **Leeuwarden** | €13.21 | **Het Hogeland** | €225,000 | €2,151 |\n| **Enschede** | €13.66 | **Kerkrade** | €249,000 | €2,040 |\n| **Heerlen** | €12.34 | ****Pekela**** | €237,000 | €1,995 |\n\n\\*As of January 2026\n\nYou can read more about the real estate market and property prices in our articles on [renting](https://www.expatica.com/nl/housing/renting/renting-a-property-in-the-netherlands-102925/) and [buying property in the Netherlands](https://www.expatica.com/nl/housing/buying/buying-a-property-in-the-netherlands-100633/). Our country article on [where to live](https://www.expatica.com/nl/moving/location/netherlands-cities-188547/) highlights popular cities for expats.\n\n![#](https://admin.expatica.com/nl/wp-content/uploads/sites/3/2022/08/netherlands-cities.jpg)\n\n[Moving\n\nWhere to live in the Netherlands: the best cities for expats\n\n[Read more](https://www.expatica.com/nl/moving/location/netherlands-cities-188547/)](https://www.expatica.com/nl/moving/location/netherlands-cities-188547/)\n\n#### Utility bills: water, gas, electricity\n\nUtility prices in the Netherlands are both higher and lower than those of other EU countries. For example, gas costs slightly less than the EU average, while water and electricity cost more.\n\n![Two wind turbines located near a field of red tulips in Lelystad, the Netherlands.](https://admin.expatica.com/nl/wp-content/uploads/sites/3/2024/09/lelystad-wind-turbine-tulips.jpg)\n\nLelystad, the Netherlands (Photo: Martijn Baudoin/Unsplash)\n\nDutch households pay, on average, [€2.61/cubic meter](https://www.drinkwaterplatform.nl/de-drinkwaterprijs-5-vragen/) for water. During the first half of 2025, gas cost [€0.0745 kilowatt/hour](https://ec.europa.eu/eurostat/databrowser/view/nrg_pc_202/default/table?lang=en&category=nrg.nrg_price.nrg_pc) (EU average: €0.0788). The average price for electricity was [€0.2660 kilowatt/hour](https://ec.europa.eu/eurostat/databrowser/view/nrg_pc_204/default/table?lang=en) (EU average: €0.2079).\n\nIn total, you can expect to pay around [€150–400](https://www.numbeo.com/cost-of-living/country_result.jsp?country=Netherlands) on utility costs, depending on your consumption and property size. Our article on [utilities in the Netherlands](https://www.expatica.com/nl/living/household/utilities-netherlands-100581/) explains the topic in more detail.\n\n![#](https://admin.expatica.com/nl/wp-content/uploads/sites/3/2023/11/utilities-netherlands.jpg)\n\n[Living\n\nSetting up utilities in the Netherlands\n\n[Read more](https://www.expatica.com/nl/living/household/utilities-netherlands-100581/)](https://www.expatica.com/nl/living/household/utilities-netherlands-100581/)\n\n### The cost of healthcare in the Netherlands\n\nEveryone living and working in the Netherlands must have [health insurance](https://www.expatica.com/nl/healthcare/healthcare-basics/a-guide-to-health-insurance-in-the-netherlands-109293/). The country’s [public healthcare system](https://www.expatica.com/nl/healthcare/healthcare-basics/healthcare-in-the-netherlands-100057/) has (almost) universal coverage, though some copayments are required for prescription meds and certain medical procedures.\n\nMany locals and expats top up their mandatory insurance policy with additional coverage. This allows for shorter waiting times, a wider choice of doctors and specialists, and access to other healthcare services, such as dentistry or vision care.\n\nBasic insurance packages average around €159 per month (2026). The more comprehensive the plan, the higher the cost will be. Parents and older adults may face higher premiums depending on their needs.\n\n![#](https://admin.expatica.com/nl/wp-content/uploads/sites/3/2023/11/health-insurance-netherlands.jpg)\n\n[Healthcare\n\nGuide to getting health insurance in the Netherlands\n\n[Read more](https://www.expatica.com/nl/healthcare/healthcare-basics/a-guide-to-health-insurance-in-the-netherlands-109293/)](https://www.expatica.com/nl/healthcare/healthcare-basics/a-guide-to-health-insurance-in-the-netherlands-109293/)\n\n### Internet and telecom prices in 2026\n\nWhile [relatively expensive,](https://digital-strategy.ec.europa.eu/en/library/mobile-and-fixed-broadband-prices-europe-2022) the cost of internet and other telecommunication services in the Netherlands differs greatly depending on the provider and the type of connection or service.\n\nFor example, standard telecom packages that include [internet, TV, and home phone](https://www.expatica.com/nl/living/household/internet-in-the-netherlands-445246/) cost more than internet-only plans. Likewise, some companies offer a free [SIM card or mobile phone plan](https://www.expatica.com/nl/living/household/internet-in-the-netherlands-445246/) if you purchase the more expensive telecom package.\n\n![Man with dreadlocks and a suit looking down at his phone. He's pulling off his headphones and has one foot resting on his skateboard.](https://admin.expatica.com/nl/wp-content/uploads/sites/3/2024/09/dreadlocks-phone-skateboard.jpg)\n\nPhoto: Getty Images via Unsplash\n\nYou can install fiber broadband starting from €27.50 a month, though your internet connection might not be great. Of course, the price rises when you want to increase the speed or add (more) TV channels. Before you sign up for a deal, make sure you compare telecom plans on an online platform such as [Independer.](https://www.independer.nl/telecom/internet-tv/intro.aspx)\n\nIn 2026, the average communication costs in the Netherlands will amount to €133 per month.\n\n![#](https://admin.expatica.com/nl/wp-content/uploads/sites/3/2014/05/internet-netherlands.jpg)\n\n[Living\n\nSetting up TV, home phone, and internet in the Netherlands\n\n[Read more](https://www.expatica.com/nl/living/household/internet-in-the-netherlands-445246/)](https://www.expatica.com/nl/living/household/internet-in-the-netherlands-445246/)\n\n![#](https://admin.expatica.com/nl/wp-content/uploads/sites/3/2014/05/netherlands-sim-card.jpg)\n\n[Living\n\nHow to get a phone number and a SIM card in the Netherlands\n\n[Read more](https://www.expatica.com/nl/living/household/netherlands-sim-cards-and-mobile-phones-1026213/)](https://www.expatica.com/nl/living/household/netherlands-sim-cards-and-mobile-phones-1026213/)\n\n### The cost of clothing and footwear\n\nClothing and footwear in the Netherlands are fairly similarly priced to those in surrounding countries. In 2026, households are expected to spend an average of €148 per month on their wardrobe.\n\nThe exact price varies greatly, of course, depending on the type and brand. For example, underwear costs around €3–10. Dresses and jeans, on the other hand, cost between €20–120. Business shoes for men and women range from €60–200.\n\nNaturally, designer clothes and accessories are much more expensive.\n\n![Woman with a RBF concentrating while she's checking out vintage clothing at the market.](https://admin.expatica.com/nl/wp-content/uploads/sites/3/2024/09/clothing-market-second-hand.jpg)\n\nAmsterdam (Photo: Ana Fernandez/SOPA Images/LightRocket via Getty Images)\n\n### The cost of transportation in the Netherlands\n\nIn 2026, people will spend an average of €425 per month on transportation. This makes it the third largest cost of living expense in the Netherlands, after housing and groceries.\n\n#### Public transport\n\nThe Netherlands has an excellent [public transportation network](https://www.expatica.com/nl/living/transportation/guide-to-public-transport-in-the-netherlands-100594/) of buses and trains, and sometimes metros and trams. There are a couple of different providers, but fares are relatively consistent throughout the country. According to [Numbeo’s cost of living in the Netherlands,](https://www.numbeo.com/cost-of-living/country_result.jsp?country=Netherlands) a single bus fare costs €2.–5, depending on the duration of the trip.\n\nIf you are planning to commute to work, it’s recommended you get a yearly plan to travel at a discounted cost. For example, the NS (i.e., the national train service) has multiple subscription plans for free or reduced travel costs outside rush hour.\n\n![#](https://admin.expatica.com/nl/wp-content/uploads/sites/3/2023/11/netherlands-transportation.jpg)\n\n[Living\n\nPublic transport in the Netherlands\n\n[Read more](https://www.expatica.com/nl/living/transportation/guide-to-public-transport-in-the-netherlands-100594/)](https://www.expatica.com/nl/living/transportation/guide-to-public-transport-in-the-netherlands-100594/)\n\n#### Driving and vehicle costs\n\nDriving your own vehicle is a costly business in the Netherlands. According to the [2025 Car Cost Index,](https://www.ayvens.com/-/media/ayvens/public/shared/documents/ayvens-car-cost-index-2025---march-2025.pdf) Dutch drivers pay an average of €1,026 a year.\n\n![Gold car parked along the canal in Amsterdam.](https://admin.expatica.com/nl/wp-content/uploads/sites/3/2024/09/gold-car-amsterdam.jpg)\n\nAmsterdam (Photo: Ugur Arpaci/Unsplash)\n\nBeyond the costs of lessons, a [driving license](https://www.expatica.com/nl/living/transportation/dutch-driving-license-107660/), and the [actual purchase of the vehicle](https://www.expatica.com/nl/living/transportation/buying-a-car-in-the-netherlands-1986046/), you’ll need to budget for:\n\n* **[Car insurance](https://www.expatica.com/nl/finance/insurance/car-insurance-in-the-netherlands-115185/)** – the mandatory Third Party Liability insurance starts from €35 a month\n* **Quarterly Road Tax** – the amount is largely based on your vehicle and fuel type\n* **[Fuel costs](https://www.globalpetrolprices.com/Netherlands/)** – as of January 2026, gasoline costs €1.99 per liter, and diesel costs €1.77 per liter\n* **Maintenance** – cars must routinely pass inspection, which usually costs around €25–70 depending on the fuel type\n\nLearn more about the costs, mandatory insurance, and driving regulations by reading our article on [driving in the Netherlands](https://www.expatica.com/nl/living/transportation/driving-and-parking-rules-in-the-netherlands-100583/).\n\n### How much does leisure and entertainment cost?\n\nIn 2026, Dutch households will spend roughly €322 on culture and recreation, and a further €178 on hospitality and restaurants.\n\n#### Movie theaters and cinemas\n\nThe average cinema ticket in the Netherlands costs around €12–17, depending on the location. Some movies, like 3D, are subject to a surcharge. Likewise, some movie theaters offer discounted tickets for young persons, students, and seniors over 65.\n\n#### Restaurants\n\nThe Netherlands boasts [119 Michelin star restaurants,](https://guide.michelin.com/nl/nl/article/michelin-guide-ceremony/de-volledige-lijst-van-de-sterren-in-de-michelin-gids-nederland-2023) and a date night there can set you back quite a bit. However, depending on your tastes and location, dining out can be quite affordable.\n\nA meal at a modest restaurant costs roughly €12–30, excluding drinks. A three-course meal at a mid-range restaurant, on the other hand, can set you back €80 for two.\n\nTips are usually included in the sales tax. However, if you are feeling generous, you can leave a 5–10% tip.\n\n![Two women sitting at an Asian restaurant having dinner.](https://admin.expatica.com/nl/wp-content/uploads/sites/3/2024/09/asian-restaurant-date.jpg)\n\nPhoto: Rendy Novantino/Unsplash\n\n#### Sports\n\nA gym membership will set you back €20–60 per month, depending on your location and sporting needs. Before you sign up for a plan, it’s worth trying out multiple gyms. Some also offer day passes that allow you to save on a monthly membership.\n\n### The cost of Dutch education and childcare\n\n#### Childcare\n\nWorking parents with (young) children may need to arrange for childcare from time to time. In the Netherlands, finding an affordable solution can be challenging. Options include crèches, daycares (*kinderopvang*), and independent childminders.\n\nThe Dutch government has set a maximum hourly rate for public childcare facilities. In 2026, these arre [€8.49–11.23 per hour,](https://www.rijksoverheid.nl/onderwerpen/kinderopvangtoeslag/bedragen-kinderopvangtoeslag-2024) depending on their services. However, childcare providers may charge higher fees; parents are expected to pay these themselves.\n\nYou can also send your child to private or international childcare, where average rates go up to €13,20 an hour. While uncommon, some expat families choose to hire a nanny or au pair. Nannies have an [average hourly rate of €17,](https://www.erieri.com/salary/job/nanny/netherlands) whereas au pairs require room and board, as well as a minimum salary of [€300–340 a month.](https://www.aupairworld.com/en/au-pair-programs/netherlands/general-conditions)\n\nVisit our article on [childcare in the Netherlands](https://www.expatica.com/nl/living/family/childcare-in-the-netherlands-102203/) to learn more about available options, as well as childcare benefits.\n\n![#](https://admin.expatica.com/nl/wp-content/uploads/sites/3/2023/11/childcare-netherlands.jpg)\n\n[Living\n\nChildcare in the Netherlands\n\n[Read more](https://www.expatica.com/nl/living/family/childcare-in-the-netherlands-102203/)](https://www.expatica.com/nl/living/family/childcare-in-the-netherlands-102203/)\n\n#### Children’s education\n\n[Public schools in the Netherlands](https://www.expatica.com/nl/education/children-education/dutch-education-system-100816/) are funded by the government and don’t charge tuition fees. Parents may be asked for a “voluntary” contribution (*eigen bijdrage* – no, it’s not voluntary unless you want your child to miss out) to pay for outings. Classes are taught in Dutch, and extra lessons will be made available if your child isn’t proficient.\n\nExpat families often prefer to send their kids to a private or international school. These come with hefty school fees, which depend on your child’s age, the school’s location, and the curriculum. For example, the cost of international primary and secondary schools ranges from €5,000–€22,000 per year.\n\nDiscover more about the costs and curriculums by reading our article on [international schools in the Netherlands](https://www.expatica.com/nl/education/children-education/international-school-in-the-netherlands-102018/).\n\n![#](https://admin.expatica.com/nl/wp-content/uploads/sites/3/2014/05/international-school-netherlands.jpg)\n\n[Education\n\nInternational schools in the Netherlands\n\n[Read more](https://www.expatica.com/nl/education/children-education/international-school-in-the-netherlands-102018/)](https://www.expatica.com/nl/education/children-education/international-school-in-the-netherlands-102018/)\n\nMany international schools in the Netherlands require payment in euros, which can be challenging if your income is in another currency.\n\nWhen paying annual or semester fees, the exchange rate and transfer fees can significantly impact the total cost. For example, a €15,000 annual fee could cost hundreds of euros more depending on the exchange rate markup applied by banks.\n\n#### University tuition fees\n\nWhile not completely unreasonably priced, universities in the Netherlands are much more expensive than in neighboring countries like Belgium, France, Germany, Italy, and Sweden.\n\nRegular bachelor and master courses at a public [university in the Netherlands](https://www.expatica.com/nl/education/higher-education/universities-in-netherlands-240277/) cost €2,601 per year for EU students in 2025/26 (rising to €2,694 for 2026/27), though charge higher fees for non-EU students. Private universities (e.g., MBA programs) average between €7,000 and €70,000 a year.\n\nOur article on [studying in the Netherlands](https://www.expatica.com/nl/education/higher-education/study-in-the-netherlands-104928/) covers this topic in more detail.\n\n![#](https://admin.expatica.com/nl/wp-content/uploads/sites/3/2023/11/study-in-the-netherlands.jpg)\n\n[Education\n\nStudy in the Netherlands\n\n[Read more](https://www.expatica.com/nl/education/higher-education/study-in-the-netherlands-104928/)](https://www.expatica.com/nl/education/higher-education/study-in-the-netherlands-104928/)\n\n## How does the Netherlands compare to the rest of the world?\n\nThe Netherlands performs particularly well in the [OECD Better Life Index,](https://www.oecdbetterlifeindex.org/countries/netherlands/) which measures standards of living. The country shines in the fields of civic engagement, education, employment, environmental quality, life satisfaction, safety, social networks, and work-life balance.\n\nLikewise, the [Human Development Index](https://hdr.undp.org/data-center/human-development-index#/indicies/HDI) ranks the Netherlands as the 8th best in the world.\n\n![People chilling on the Plein, in The Hague.](https://admin.expatica.com/nl/wp-content/uploads/sites/3/2023/11/plein-den-haag.jpg)\n\nThe town square (Plein) in The Hague, the Netherlands (Photo: Madrabothair/Dreamstime)\n\nAmsterdam is the city with the highest rank on the [2024 Mercer’s Quality of Living Index](https://mobilityexchange.mercer.com/insights/quality-of-living-rankings) (6th). It stands below places like Zurich (1st) and Copenhagen (4th) but above Sydney (12th) and Berlin (19th). Meanwhile, The Hague ranks (14th), outperforming other major cities like Paris (34th), Brussels (40th), London (40th), and Rome (59th).\n\nRegardless of which Dutch city you choose, managing your money efficiently across currencies is essential for expats. From paying your monthly rent to everyday purchases at Albert Heijn, the exchange rate you get affects your actual cost of living. **[Wise](https://wise.com/nl/account/?utm_source=expatica-com&utm_medium=affiliate&utm_campaign=guides&utm_content=nl/moving/about/cost-of-living-in-netherlands-1085103/)** helps expats manage their finances with low transparent fees, and currency conversion at the mid-market exchange rate.\n\nYou can hold 40+ currencies in your [Wise Account](https://wise.com/nl/account/?utm_source=expatica-com&utm_medium=affiliate&utm_campaign=guides&utm_content=nl/moving/about/cost-of-living-in-netherlands-1085103/), including euros and spend easily with the linked Wise debit card.\n\n[Open a Wise Account](https://wise.com/nl/account/?utm_source=expatica-com&utm_medium=affiliate&utm_campaign=guides&utm_content=nl/moving/about/cost-of-living-in-netherlands-1085103/)\n\n### The cost of living in Amsterdam\n\nAmsterdam is the capital of the Netherlands, home to roughly [931,748 residents](https://onderzoek.amsterdam.nl/artikel/bevolking-in-cijfers-2024) (2024). Every year, it draws 10,000s of hopefuls who want to make the city their stomping ground. This, of course, also explains the exorbitantly high housing prices.\n\nCompared to other capital cities, [Amsterdam’s cost of living](https://www.numbeo.com/cost-of-living/in/Amsterdam) is (as of January 2026):\n\n* 31% cheaper than New York (the US)\n* 12% cheaper than London (the UK)\n* 30% more expensive than Berlin (Germany)\n* 71.% more expensive than Warsaw (Poland)\n* 147% more expensive than Ankara (Türkiye)\n* 347% more expensive than Delhi (India)\n\n### The cost of living in Rotterdam\n\nAs the second largest city in the Netherlands, Rotterdam is an urban hotspot that attracts increasingly more expats. After sustaining catastrophic damage during World War II (WW2), the city literally rose from the ashes and rebuilt itself as one of the country’s most architecturally innovative and experimental places.\n\n![Man holding his young child and paying an oliebollenkraam using his phone.](https://admin.expatica.com/nl/wp-content/uploads/sites/3/2017/05/netherlands-oliebollenkraam-payment.jpg)\n\nPhoto: Lorado/Getty Images\n\nAs of January 2026, the [cost of living in Rotterdam](https://www.numbeo.com/cost-of-living/in/Rotterdam) is:\n\n* 42% cheaper than New York (the US)\n* 27% cheaper than London (the UK)\n* 17% cheaper than Amsterdam\n* 8% more expensive than Berlin (Germany)\n* 42% more expensive than Warsaw (Poland)\n* 106% more expensive than Ankara (Türkiye)\n* 272% more expensive than Delhi (India)\n\n### The cost of living in The Hague\n\nAs the country’s political center and home to the International Court of Justice, The Hague offers a good quality of life and great access to international schools. The city is also close to the Netherlands’ most popular beach, Scheveningen.\n\nThe [cost of living in The Hague](https://www.numbeo.com/cost-of-living/in/The-Hague-Den-Haag-Netherlands) is typically cheaper than in Amsterdam or Rotterdam, as well as:\n\n* 41% cheaper than New York (the US)\n* 26% cheaper than London (the UK)\n* 16% cheaper than Amsterdam\n* 10% more expensive than Berlin (Germany)\n* 44% more expensive than Warsaw (Poland)\n* 109% more expensive than Ankara (Türkiye)\n* 277% more expensive than Delhi (India)\n\n### The cost of living in Utrecht\n\nUtrecht’s central location and proximity to Amsterdam, excellent transport links, and large student population have made this the fourth-largest city in the Netherlands. Known for its historic city center, Utrecht has a diverse cultural scene with interesting museums and lively festivals.\n\n![Utrecht on a sunny day.](https://admin.expatica.com/nl/wp-content/uploads/sites/3/2024/09/utrecht-sunny-day.jpg)\n\nUtrecht, the Netherlands (Photo: Matt Mutlu/Unsplash)\n\nAs of January 2026, the [cost of living in Utrecht](https://www.numbeo.com/cost-of-living/in/Utrecht) is:\n\n* 40% cheaper than New York (the US)\n* 24% cheaper than London (the UK)\n* 14% cheaper than Amsterdam\n* 12% more expensive than Berlin (Germany)\n* 47% more expensive than Warsaw (Poland)\n* 114% more expensive than Ankara (Türkiye)\n* 287% more expensive than Delhi (India)\n\n## Financial support for the cost of living in the Netherlands\n\nThe high cost of living in the Netherlands is a major point of concern, especially among voters. In [September 2024,](https://www.rijksoverheid.nl/actueel/nieuws/2024/09/17/de-belangrijkste-plannen-van-prinsjesdag-2024-in-eenvoudige-taal) the new [government](https://www.expatica.com/nl/living/gov-law-admin/netherlands-political-system-100756/) declared it would increase social security benefits, lower income taxes, and reduce the aid sent to underdeveloped economies.\n\nHowever, housing costs keep rising, and health insurance costs €159 on average in 2026. As such, it’s unsure what the future will bring in terms of savings and purchasing power.\n\nIf you need help covering the cost of living in the Netherlands and you are eligible, you can apply for [social security benefits](https://www.expatica.com/nl/living/gov-law-admin/social-security-in-the-netherlands-100578/). For example, low-income households can apply for a [social benefit](https://www.rijksoverheid.nl/onderwerpen/bijstand) (*bijstandsuitkering*).\n\nOther benefits that can help you with the cost of living in the Netherlands:\n\n* **[Child benefits](https://ec.europa.eu/social/main.jsp?catId=1122&langId=en&intPageId=4985)** – to help with the cost of raising a child or [childcare](https://ec.europa.eu/social/main.jsp?catId=1122&langId=en&intPageId=4986)\n* **[Invalidity benefits](https://ec.europa.eu/social/main.jsp?catId=1122&langId=en&intPageId=4990)**– for employees who are partially or fully incapacitated to work\n* **[Long-term care benefits](https://ec.europa.eu/social/main.jsp?catId=1122&langId=en&intPageId=4989)** – for dependent, disabled, or elderly persons who require specific equipment or long-term care\n* **[Sickness pay](https://ec.europa.eu/social/main.jsp?catId=1122&langId=en&intPageId=4992)** – available to workers who are unable to work due to an illness or an accident\n* **[Survivor’s pension](https://ec.europa.eu/social/main.jsp?catId=1122&langId=en&intPageId=4994)** – for dependents who lose their parents or (ex)partner\n* **[Unemployment benefits](https://ec.europa.eu/social/main.jsp?catId=1122&langId=en&intPageId=4996)** – to help people who are in between jobs\n\nIf you are unable to pay for food and groceries, you can contact the local [food bank](https://voedselbankennederland.nl/ik-zoek-hulp/locaties/) (*voedselbank*) for support.\n\n## Tips on how to save money in the Netherlands\n\nAll affordability is relative to your household income and size. A single bachelor may have more to spend than a family of four. Here are some tips for saving money while living in the Netherlands:\n\n* **Shop smartly at local markets** – local markets often have fresh produce, bread, and other goods at a lower price than supermarkets. Time your visit well: there are better deals around closing time when vendors try to clear their stock.\n* **Check the weekly ads and plan meals ahead** – use the weekly advertising flyers (*reclamefolders*) to see which supermarket has the best deals. You can save more money by planning ahead, as Dutch supermarkets often have promotions like “1+1 free” on bulk items.\n* **Dine out strategically** – when you are eating out, look for *daghap* specials, as these are often more affordable than other dinner items. There are also many discount platforms (such as [Social Deal)](https://www.socialdeal.nl/#join) that offer bargain outings.\n* **Embrace cycling, public transport, and ride-sharing services** – the Netherlands has a well-connected public transport system and is bike-friendly. Opt for a monthly or yearly transport pass or use a bike instead of driving. For longer commutes, consider carpooling with colleagues or friends or using a ride-sharing app to lower your travel costs.\n* **Shop at second-hand stores** – the Netherlands has many thrift stores (like *Rataplan*) where you can find affordable clothing, furniture, and household items. It’s also eco-friendly!\n* **Use energy wisely** – save on heating and electricity by insulating your home, using energy-efficient appliances, and taking advantage of off-peak energy tariffs (e.g., to do your washing). You can also lower the thermostat a little and wear double layers inside the home.\n* **Review your contracts** – regularly compare energy, internet, and phone plans to ensure you’re getting the best deal.\n* **Be smart about currency exchange** – If you’re regularly transferring money to or from the Netherlands, or managing expenses in multiple currencies, the exchange rate you get matters. Consider using services like [Wise](https://wise.com/nl/account/?utm_source=expatica-com&utm_medium=affiliate&utm_campaign=guides&utm_content=nl/moving/about/cost-of-living-in-netherlands-1085103/) that use the mid-market exchange rate – this can help reduce costs when receiving your salary, paying bills, or sending money abroad. Always compare the total cost (exchange rate + fees) rather than just looking at transfer fees alone.\n\nUseful resources\n\n* [Consumers Union](https://www.consumentenbond.nl/) (*Consumentenbond*) – official website of the Netherlands’ independent watchdog for consumers\n* [Food Banks Netherlands](https://voedselbankennederland.nl/ik-zoek-hulp/locaties/) (*Voedselbanken Nederland*) – website with information on food banks in the Netherlands\n* [Numbeo](https://www.numbeo.com/cost-of-living/country_result.jsp?country=Netherlands) – information on the cost of living in the Netherlands\n\n[![Laura van der Most](https://admin.expatica.com/nl/wp-content/uploads/2023/11/laura-vandermost-150x150.jpg)](https://www.expatica.com/authors/laura-van-der-most/)[Laura van der Most](https://www.expatica.com/authors/laura-van-der-most/)\n\n## About the author\n\nOriginally from the Netherlands, Laura speaks Dutch, English, *un peu* French, and *sehr schlechtes* German.\n\nAfter studying English literature, she discovered a passion for editing and has been working on improving texts ever since.       "title": "Rotterdam - Wikipedia",
      "content": "Rotterdam (/ˈrɒtərdæm/ ROT-ər-dam, UK also /ˌrɒtərˈdæm/ ROT-ər-DAM; Dutch: ( \"Help:IPA/Dutch\") ⓘ) is the second-largest city in the Netherlands by population and the largest by area (319.4 km2). It is in the province of South Holland, part of the North Sea mouth of the Rhine–Meuse–Scheldt delta, via the New Meuse inland shipping channel, dug to connect to the Meuse at first and now to the Rhine.\n\nRotterdam's history goes back to 1270, when a dam was constructed in the Rotte \"Rotte (river)\"). In 1340, Rotterdam was granted city rights by William IV, Count of Holland. The Rotterdam–The Hague metropolitan area, with a population of approximately 2.7 million, is the 10th-largest in the European Union and the most populous in the country. Wikidata](https://www.wikidata.org/wiki/Q34370#P856 \"Edit this at Wikidata\") (in Dutch) |\n\n**Rotterdam** ([/ˈrɒtərdæm/](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help:IPA/English \"Help:IPA/English\") [*ROT-ər-dam*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help:Pronunciation_respelling_key \"Help:Pronunciation respelling key\"), [UK](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_English \"British English\") also [/ˌrɒtərˈdæm/](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help:IPA/English \"Help:IPA/English\") [*ROT-ər-DAM*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help:Pronunciation_respelling_key \"Help:Pronunciation respelling key\");[[8]](#cite_note-8)[[9]](#cite_note-9) Dutch: [[ˌrɔtərˈdɑm]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help:IPA/Dutch \"Help:IPA/Dutch\") [ⓘ](/wiki/File:Nl-Rotterdam.ogg \"File:Nl-Rotterdam.ogg\"))[[10]](#cite_note-10) is the second-largest [city](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_cities_in_the_Netherlands_by_province \"List of cities in the Netherlands by province\") in the [Netherlands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Netherlands \"Netherlands\") by population and the largest by area (319.4 km2). It is in the [province](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Provinces_of_the_Netherlands \"Provinces of the Netherlands\") of [South Holland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Holland \"South Holland\"), part of the [North Sea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Sea \"North Sea\") mouth of the [Rhine–Meuse–Scheldt delta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rhine–Meuse–Scheldt_delta \"Rhine–Meuse–Scheldt delta\"), via the [New Meuse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nieuwe_Maas \"Nieuwe Maas\") inland shipping channel, dug to connect to the [Meuse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meuse \"Meuse\") at first and now to the [Rhine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rhine \"Rhine\").\n\nRotterdam's history goes back to 1270, when a [dam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dam \"Dam\") was constructed in the [Rotte](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rotte_(river) \"Rotte (river)\"). In 1340, Rotterdam was granted city rights by [William IV, Count of Holland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_II,_Count_of_Hainaut \"William II, Count of Hainaut\"). The [Rotterdam–The Hague metropolitan area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rotterdam–The_Hague_metropolitan_area \"Rotterdam–The Hague metropolitan area\"), with a population of approximately 2.7 million, is the [10th-largest in the European Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_urban_areas_in_the_European_Union \"List of urban areas in the European Union\") and the most populous in the country.\n\nA major logistic and economic centre, Rotterdam is [Europe's largest seaport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_of_Rotterdam \"Port of Rotterdam\"). In 2022, Rotterdam had a population of 655,468 and is home to over 180 different nationalities.[[11]](#cite_note-11)\n\nRotterdam is known for its [university](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Erasmus_University_Rotterdam \"Erasmus University Rotterdam\"), riverside setting, lively cultural life, maritime heritage and modern [architecture](#Architecture). The near-complete destruction of the city centre during the [World War II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_War_II \"World War II\") [German bombing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_bombing_of_Rotterdam \"German bombing of Rotterdam\") has resulted in a varied architectural landscape, including [skyscrapers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Skyscraper \"Skyscraper\") designed by architects such as [Rem Koolhaas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rem_Koolhaas \"Rem Koolhaas\"), [Piet Blom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Piet_Blom \"Piet Blom\") and [Ben van Berkel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ben_van_Berkel \"Ben van Berkel\").[[12]](#cite_note-12)[[13]](#cite_note-Academy_of_Urbanism-13)[[14]](#cite_note-14)\n\nThe [Rhine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rhine \"Rhine\"), [Meuse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meuse \"Meuse\") and [Scheldt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scheldt \"Scheldt\") give waterway access into the heart of Western Europe, including the highly industrialized [Ruhr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ruhr \"Ruhr\"). The extensive distribution system including rail, roads, and waterways have earned Rotterdam the nicknames \"Gateway to Europe\" and \"Gateway to the World\".[[15]](#cite_note-15)[[16]](#cite_note-16)[[17]](#cite_note-17)\n\n## History\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Rotterdam&action=edit&section=1 \"Edit section: History\")]\n\nFor a chronological guide, see [Timeline of Rotterdam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_Rotterdam \"Timeline of Rotterdam\").\n\n### Early history\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Rotterdam&action=edit&section=2 \"Edit section: Early history\")]\n\n[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c1/Rotterdam_Map_by_Frederick_De_Wit_c1690.jpg/250px-Rotterdam_Map_by_Frederick_De_Wit_c1690.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Rotterdam_Map_by_Frederick_De_Wit_c1690.jpg)\n\nMap of Rotterdam by [Frederick de Wit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frederick_de_Wit \"Frederick de Wit\") (c. 1690)\n\nThe settlement at the lower end of the [fen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fen \"Fen\") stream *[Rotte](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rotte_(river) \"Rotte (river)\")* (or *Rotta*, as it was then known, from *rot*, \"muddy\", and *a*, \"water\", thus \"muddy water\") dates from at least the year 950.[[18]](#cite_note-18) Around 1150, large [floods](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flood \"Flood\") in the area ended development, leading to the construction of protective [dikes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Levee \"Levee\") and dams, including *Schielands Hoge Zeedijk* (\"Schieland's High Sea Dike\") along the northern banks of the present-day [Nieuwe Maas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nieuwe_Maas \"Nieuwe Maas\") river. A dam on the Rotte was built in the 1260s and was located at the present-day *Hoogstraat*.[[19]](#cite_note-Bokern-19)\n\nOn 7 July 1340, Count [Willem IV of Holland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_II_of_Hainaut \"William II of Hainaut\") granted [city rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/City_rights_in_the_Low_Countries \"City rights in the Low Countries\") to Rotterdam, which then had a population of only a few thousand.[[20]](#cite_note-20) Around the year 1350, a shipping canal (the *[Rotterdamse Schie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Schie \"Schie\")*) was completed, which provided Rotterdam access to the larger towns in the north, allowing it to become a local trans-shipment centre between the Netherlands, [England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/England \"England\") and [Germany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Germany \"Germany\"), and to [urbanize](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urbanization \"Urbanization\").[[21]](#cite_note-21) However, the economy of Rotterdam was mainly based on herring fishing until the [Eighty Years' War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eighty_Years'_War \"Eighty Years' War\"), when it turned out that it is one of the few Dutch towns with an easy sea access. As a result, [William the Silent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_the_Silent \"William the Silent\") built new fortifications around Rotterdam.[[19]](#cite_note-Bokern-19)\n\nBeginning in the 1600s, Rotterdam was involved in the [Atlantic slave trade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atlantic_slave_trade \"Atlantic slave trade\"). According to historian Gerhard de Kok, \"Rotterdam merchants were the pioneers of the [Dutch slave trade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Dutch_slavery \"History of Dutch slavery\")\". From the 17th century until 1814, when the [United Netherlands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sovereign_Principality_of_the_United_Netherlands \"Sovereign Principality of the United Netherlands\") abolished the Netherlands' involvement in the slave trade at the request of the [British government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government_of_the_United_Kingdom \"Government of the United Kingdom\"), Dutch [slave ships](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slave_ship \"Slave ship\") from Rotterdam sailed to [Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Africa \"Africa\") and the [Americas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Americas \"Americas\") as part of the [triangular trade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Triangular_trade \"Triangular trade\"). Rotterdam merchants also sold significant quantities of [gunpowder](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gunpowder \"Gunpowder\") to [Zeeland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zeeland \"Zeeland\")-based slave ships.[[22]](#cite_note-22)\n\n[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e0/Detroit_Publishing_Company_-_Rotterdam_-_Delftsevaart%2C_c._1895.jpg/250px-Detroit_Publishing_Company_-_Rotterdam_-_Delftsevaart%2C_c._1895.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Detroit_Publishing_Company_-_Rotterdam_-_Delftsevaart,_c._1895.jpg)\n\nThe Delftsevaart, c. 1890–1905\n\n [![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8b/Standbeeld_Maagd_van_Nederland%2C_Nieuwe_Markt%2C_Rotterdam%2C_1915.jpg/250px-Standbeeld_Maagd_van_Nederland%2C_Nieuwe_Markt%2C_Rotterdam%2C_1915.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Standbeeld_Maagd_van_Nederland,_Nieuwe_Markt,_Rotterdam,_1915.jpg)\n\nNieuwe Markt, 1915\n\nThe port of Rotterdam grew steadily in importance, the new harbours were built, and Rotterdam became the seat of one of the six chambers of the Vereenigde Oostindische Compagnie (VOC), the [Dutch East India Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dutch_East_India_Company \"Dutch East India Company\"), and one of the five chambers of the West-Indische Compagnie (WIC), the [Dutch West India Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dutch_West_India_Company \"Dutch West India Company\").[*[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed \"Wikipedia:Citation needed\")*] In the 17th century, the city grew beyond the fortifications, and by the 1670s, its population was around 30,000.[[19]](#cite_note-Bokern-19)\n\nThe greatest spurt of growth, both in port activity and population, followed the completion of the [Nieuwe Waterweg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nieuwe_Waterweg \"Nieuwe Waterweg\") in 1872. The city and harbour started to expand on the south bank of the river. The *[Witte Huis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Witte_Huis \"Witte Huis\"),* or *White House* skyscraper,[[23]](#cite_note-23) inspired by American office buildings and built in 1898 in the French [Art Nouveau style](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Art_Nouveau \"Art Nouveau\"), is evidence of Rotterdam's rapid growth and success. When completed, it was the tallest office building in Europe, with a height of 45 m (147.64 ft).[*[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed \"Wikipedia:Citation needed\")*]\n\n### 20th century\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Rotterdam&action=edit&section=3 \"Edit section: 20th century\")]\n\n[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/61/Rotterdam%2C_Laurenskerk%2C_na_bombardement_van_mei_1940.jpg/250px-Rotterdam%2C_Laurenskerk%2C_na_bombardement_van_mei_1940.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Rotterdam,_Laurenskerk,_na_bombardement_van_mei_1940.jpg)\n\nRotterdam centre after the [1940 bombing of Rotterdam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rotterdam_Blitz \"Rotterdam Blitz\"). The ruined [St. Lawrence Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grote_or_Sint-Laurenskerk_(Rotterdam) \"Grote or Sint-Laurenskerk (Rotterdam)\") has been restored.\n\n [![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b9/Rotterdam-Kop_van_Zuid%2C_World_Portcenter%2C_Montevideo_en_hotel_New_York_foto12_2011-01-09_14.12.JPG/250px-Rotterdam-Kop_van_Zuid%2C_World_Portcenter%2C_Montevideo_en_hotel_New_York_foto12_2011-01-09_14.12.JPG?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Rotterdam-Kop_van_Zuid,_World_Portcenter,_Montevideo_en_hotel_New_York_foto12_2011-01-09_14.12.JPG)\n\n[Tower blocks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tower_block \"Tower block\") in the [Kop van Zuid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kop_van_Zuid \"Kop van Zuid\") neighbourhood\n\nDuring [World War I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_War_I \"World War I\"), the city was the world's largest spy centre because of Dutch neutrality and its strategic location between Britain, Germany and German-occupied Belgium. Many spies who were arrested and executed in Britain were led by German secret agents operating from Rotterdam. [MI6](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MI6 \"MI6\") had its main European office on de Boompjes. From there the British coordinated espionage in Germany and occupied Belgium. During World War I, an average of 25,000 Belgian refugees lived in the city, as well as hundreds of German deserters and escaped Allied prisoners of war.[[24]](#cite_note-24)\n\nDuring [World War II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_War_II \"World War II\"), the German army [invaded the Netherlands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_invasion_of_the_Netherlands \"German invasion of the Netherlands\") on 10 May 1940.[[25]](#cite_note-25) [Adolf Hitler](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adolf_Hitler \"Adolf Hitler\") had hoped to conquer the country in just one day, but his forces met unexpectedly fierce resistance. The Dutch army was forced to capitulate on 15 May 1940, following the [bombing of Rotterdam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rotterdam_Blitz \"Rotterdam Blitz\") on 14 May and the threat of bombing other Dutch cities.[[26]](#cite_note-26)[[27]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAmersfoort2005369-27)[[28]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGötzel1980149,_150-28)[[29]](#cite_note-29) The heart of Rotterdam was almost completely destroyed by the [Luftwaffe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luftwaffe \"Luftwaffe\"). Some 80,000 civilians were made homeless, and 900 were killed; a relatively low number since many had fled the city because of the warfare and bombing going on in Rotterdam since the start of the invasion three days earlier. The City Hall survived the bombing. [Ossip Zadkine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ossip_Zadkine \"Ossip Zadkine\") later attempted to capture the event with his statue *[De Verwoeste Stad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/De_Verwoeste_Stad \"De Verwoeste Stad\")* ('The Destroyed City'). The statue stands near the Leuvehaven, not far from the [Erasmusbrug](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Erasmusbrug \"Erasmusbrug\") in the centre of the city, on the north shore of the river Nieuwe Maas. In 1941, 11,000 Jews still lived in Rotterdam. Before the war there were 13,000. Between 30 July 1942 and 22 April 1943, 6,790 people were deported in 8 transports via Loods 24. The vast majority of the Jews who were deported via Loods 24 were murdered in [Sobibór](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sobibor_extermination_camp \"Sobibor extermination camp\") and [Auschwitz-Birkenau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Auschwitz_concentration_camp \"Auschwitz concentration camp\"). Research in 2000 showed that 144 people survived the deportations. In 2013 the [Jewish Children's Monument](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_Children's_Monument?action=edit&redlink=1 \"Jewish Children's Monument (page does not exist)\") [[nl](https://nl.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joods%20Kindermonument%20(Rotterdam) \"nl:Joods Kindermonument (Rotterdam)\")] was unveiled.[*[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed \"Wikipedia:Citation needed\")*]\n\nIn January 1948, [Queen Wilhelmina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wilhelmina_of_the_Netherlands \"Wilhelmina of the Netherlands\") presented the motto 'Sterker door strijd' (Stronger through effort) as part of the [coat of arms of Rotterdam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coat_of_arms_of_Rotterdam \"Coat of arms of Rotterdam\") to the city government:\n\n\"...to serve as a record of the wartime experiences of Rotterdam residents and their role in national liberation...\"\n\n—Wilhelmina of the Netherlands\n\nRotterdam was gradually rebuilt from the 1950s through to the 1970s. Because the city centre was largely destroyed, new spatial infrastructure could be built, making it an open and modern city. In 1953 the [Lijnbaan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lijnbaan \"Lijnbaan\") was opened, the first car-free shopping street in Europe. The progressive design attracted a lot of international attention, in which film and television played an important role.[[30]](#cite_note-30) The new [Central Station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rotterdam_Centraal_station \"Rotterdam Centraal station\") was completed in 1957, with the [Groothandelsgebouw](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Groothandelsgebouw \"Groothandelsgebouw\") from 1953 next to it. The [Euromast](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Euromast \"Euromast\") was erected in 1960 on the occasion of the [Floriade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Floriade_(Netherlands) \"Floriade (Netherlands)\"). From the 1980s onwards, the city councils began developing an active architectural policy. The harbours were moving westwards, and the old environment had to be reshaped. Daring and new styles of [apartments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apartment \"Apartment\"), office buildings and recreation facilities resulted in a more '[livable](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quality_of_life \"Quality of life\")' city centre with a new skyline. In the 1990s, the [Kop van Zuid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kop_van_Zuid \"Kop van Zuid\") was built on the south bank of the river as a new business centre. Rotterdam was voted 2015 European City of the Year by the Academy of Urbanism.[[13]](#cite_note-Academy_of_Urbanism-13) A profile of Rem Koolhaas in *[The Guardian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Guardian \"The Guardian\")* begins, \"If you put the last 50 years of architecture in a blender, and spat it out in building-sized chunks across the skyline, you would probably end up with something that looked a bit like Rotterdam\".[[31]](#cite_note-Wainwright_2013-31)\n\n## Geography\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Rotterdam&action=edit&section=4 \"Edit section: Geography\")]\n\n[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b6/Rotterdam-plaats-OpenTopo.jpg/500px-Rotterdam-plaats-OpenTopo.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Rotterdam-plaats-OpenTopo.jpg)\n\nTopographic map image of Rotterdam (city), as of September 2014\n\nRotterdam is divided into a northern and a southern part by the river Nieuwe Maas, connected by (from west to east): the Beneluxtunnel; the [Maastunnel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maastunnel \"Maastunnel\"); the [Erasmusbrug](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Erasmusbrug \"Erasmusbrug\"); a subway tunnel; the Willemsspoortunnel ('Willems railway tunnel'); the [Willemsbrug](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Willemsbrug \"Willemsbrug\") ('Willems Bridge') together with the Koninginnebrug ('Queen's Bridge'); and the [Van Brienenoordbrug](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Van_Brienenoordbrug \"Van Brienenoordbrug\") ('Van Brienenoord Bridge'). The former railway lift bridge [De Hef](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/De_Hef \"De Hef\") ('the Lift') is preserved as a [Rijksmonument](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rijksmonument \"Rijksmonument\") (national heritage site) in lifted position between the [Noordereiland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Noordereiland \"Noordereiland\") ('North Island') and the south of Rotterdam.\n\n[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b2/View_of_Rotterdam_from_the_Euromast%2C_Rotterdam-Centrum%2C_Rotterdam_%282023%29_08.jpg/250px-View_of_Rotterdam_from_the_Euromast%2C_Rotterdam-Centrum%2C_Rotterdam_%282023%29_08.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:View_of_Rotterdam_from_the_Euromast,_Rotterdam-Centrum,_Rotterdam_(2023)_08.jpg)\n\nView of Rotterdam from the Euromast\n\nThe city centre is located on the northern bank of the Nieuwe Maas, although recent urban development has extended the centre to parts of southern Rotterdam known as [Kop van Zuid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kop_van_Zuid \"Kop van Zuid\") ('the Head of South', i.e., the northern part of southern Rotterdam). From its inland core, Rotterdam reaches the [North Sea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Sea \"North Sea\") by a swathe of predominantly harbour area.\n\nBuilt mostly behind dikes, large parts of Rotterdam are below sea level. For instance, the Prins Alexander Polder in the northeast of Rotterdam extends 6 m (20 ft) below sea level, or rather below [Normaal Amsterdams Peil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Normaal_Amsterdams_Peil \"Normaal Amsterdams Peil\") (NAP) or 'Amsterdam Ordnance Datum'. The lowest point in the Netherlands (6.76 m (22.2 ft) below NAP) is situated just to the east of Rotterdam, in the municipality of [Nieuwerkerk aan den IJssel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nieuwerkerk_aan_den_IJssel \"Nieuwerkerk aan den IJssel\").\n\n[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/43/Rotterdam_by_Sentinel-2%2C_2018-06-30.jpg/250px-Rotterdam_by_Sentinel-2%2C_2018-06-30.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Rotterdam_by_Sentinel-2,_2018-06-30.jpg)\n\nSatellite image of Rotterdam and its port\n\nThe [Rotte](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rotte_(river) \"Rotte (river)\") river no longer joins the Nieuwe Maas directly. Since the early 1980s, when the construction of Rotterdam's second underground line interfered with the Rotte's course, its waters have been pumped through a pipe into the [Nieuwe Maas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nieuwe_Maas \"Nieuwe Maas\") via the Boerengat.\n\n[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2b/Metropoolregio_Rotterdam_Den_Haag_%28Rotterdam_The_Hague_Metropolitan_Area%29.svg/250px-Metropoolregio_Rotterdam_Den_Haag_%28Rotterdam_The_Hague_Metropolitan_Area%29.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Metropoolregio_Rotterdam_Den_Haag_(Rotterdam_The_Hague_Metropolitan_Area).svg)\n\nThe 24 municipalities of the [Rotterdam–The Hague metropolitan area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rotterdam–The_Hague_metropolitan_area \"Rotterdam–The Hague metropolitan area\")\n\nBetween the summers of 2003 and 2008, an artificial [beach](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beach \"Beach\") was created at the Boompjeskade along the Nieuwe Maas, between the Erasmus Bridge and the Willems Bridge. [Swimming](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_swimming \"Human swimming\") was not possible; digging pits was limited to the height of the layer of [sand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sand \"Sand\"), about 50 cm (20 in). Alternatively, people go to the beach of [Hook of Holland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hook_of_Holland \"Hook of Holland\") (which is a Rotterdam district) or one of the beaches in [Zeeland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zeeland \"Zeeland\"): [Renesse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Renesse \"Renesse\") or the Zuid-Hollandse Eilanden: [Ouddorp](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ouddorp \"Ouddorp\"), [Oostvoorne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oostvoorne \"Oostvoorne\").\n\nRotterdam forms the centre of the [Rijnmond](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rijnmond \"Rijnmond\") conurbation, bordering the conurbation surrounding [The Hague](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Hague \"The Hague\") to the northwest. The two conurbations are close enough to be a single conurbation. They share the [Rotterdam The Hague Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rotterdam_The_Hague_Airport \"Rotterdam The Hague Airport\") and a light rail system called [RandstadRail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/RandstadRail \"RandstadRail\"). Consideration is being given to creating an official Metropolitan region Rotterdam The Hague (*Metropoolregio Rotterdam Den Haag*), which would have a combined population approaching 2.5 million.[*[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed \"Wikipedia:Citation needed\")*]\n\nIn its turn, the Rijnmond conurbation is part of the southern wing (the [Zuidvleugel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zuidvleugel \"Zuidvleugel\")) of the [Randstad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Randstad \"Randstad\"), which is one of the most important economic and densely populated areas in the northwest of Europe. Having a population of 7.1 million, the Randstad is the [sixth-largest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_metropolitan_areas_in_Europe \"List of metropolitan areas in Europe\") [urban area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urban_area \"Urban area\") in [Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Europe \"Europe\") (after Moscow, London, Paris, Istanbul, and the Rhein-Ruhr Area). The Zuidvleugel, situated in the province of [South Holland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Holland \"South Holland\"), has a population of around 3 million.[*[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed \"Wikipedia:Citation needed\")*]\n\n### Climate\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Rotterdam&action=edit&section=5 \"Edit section: Climate\")]\n\nRotterdam experiences a temperate [oceanic climate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oceanic_climate \"Oceanic climate\") ([Köppen climate classification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Köppen_climate_classification \"Köppen climate classification\") *Cfb*) similar to all of the coastal areas in the Netherlands. Located near to the coast, its climate is slightly milder than locations further inland. Winters are cool with frequent cold days, while the summers are mild to warm, with occasional hot temperatures. Temperature rises above 30 °C on average 4 days each summer, while (night) temperatures can drop below −5 °C during winter for short periods of time, mostly during periods of sustained easterly (continental) winds. Precipitation is generally moderate throughout the year, although spring and summer (particularly before August) are relatively drier and sunnier, while autumn and winter are cloudier with more frequent rain (or snow). The following climate data is from the airport, which is slightly cooler than the city, being surrounded by water canals which make the climate milder and with a higher [relative humidity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Relative_humidity \"Relative humidity\"). The city has an [urban heat island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urban_heat_island \"Urban heat island\"), especially inside the city centre.[[32]](#cite_note-32)\n\n| Climate data for Rotterdam (1991–2020 normals) |\n| --- |\n| Month | Jan | Feb | Mar | Apr | May | Jun | Jul | Aug | Sep | Oct | Nov | Dec | Year |\n| Record high °C (°F) | 14.2 (57.6) | 18.7 (65.7) | 23.8 (74.8) | 28.7 (83.7) | 32.7 (90.9) | 36.9 (98.4) | 38.9 (102.0) | 35.3 (95.5) | 32.5 (90.5) | 26.0 (78.8) | 19.3 (66.7) | 15.6 (60.1) | 38.9 (102.0) |\n| Mean maximum °C (°F) | 11.9 (53.4) | 12.7 (54.9) | 16.9 (62.4) | 22.4 (72.3) | 26.2 (79.2) | 29.2 (84.6) | 30.7 (87.3) | 30.1 (86.2) | 25.4 (77.7) | 20.9 (69.6) | 15.6 (60.1) | 12.5 (54.5) | 32.6 (90.7) |\n| Mean daily maximum °C (°F) | 6.4 (43.5) | 7.1 (44.8) |
