Holland\")\n\nMain article: [Transport in Rotterdam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transport_in_Rotterdam \"Transport in Rotterdam\")\n\nRotterdam offers connections by international, national, regional and local public transport systems, as well as by the Dutch [motorway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Motorway \"Motorway\") network.\n\nSeveral motorways form part of the Rotterdam ring road; the [A20](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A20_motorway_(Netherlands) \"A20 motorway (Netherlands)\") (Ring North), the [A16](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A16_motorway_(Netherlands) \"A16 motorway (Netherlands)\") (Ring East), [A15](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A15_motorway_(Netherlands) \"A15 motorway (Netherlands)\") (Ring South), and the [A4](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A4_motorway_(Netherlands) \"A4 motorway (Netherlands)\"). The following two other motorways also serve Rotterdam: the [A13](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A13_motorway_(Netherlands) \"A13 motorway (Netherlands)\") and the [A29](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A29_motorway_(Netherlands) \"A29 motorway (Netherlands)\"). Rotterdam is also home to several tunnels like the Beneluxtunnel and several bridges.\n\nRotterdam is served by one international airport. [Rotterdam The Hague Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rotterdam_The_Hague_Airport \"Rotterdam The Hague Airport\") (formerly known as *Zestienhoven*) is the third-largest [airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Airport \"Airport\") in the country. The much larger [Amsterdam Airport Schiphol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amsterdam_Airport_Schiphol \"Amsterdam Airport Schiphol\") is more frequently used by people travelling to and from Rotterdam. The airport is located 58 km (36 mi) north east of Rotterdam. [NS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nederlandse_Spoorwegen \"Nederlandse Spoorwegen\") operates direct train services between Rotterdam and Schiphol.\n\nThe main [Rotterdam Centraal station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rotterdam_Centraal_station \"Rotterdam Centraal station\") is well connected to the Dutch railway network and has several international connections. Domestic destinations include [Dordrecht](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dordrecht \"Dordrecht\"), [Breda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Breda \"Breda\"), [Eindhoven](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eindhoven \"Eindhoven\") and further south. [Flushing (Vlissingen)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flushing,_Netherlands \"Flushing, Netherlands\") in Zeeland. [The Hague](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Hague \"The Hague\"), [Amsterdam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amsterdam \"Amsterdam\") to the north west. [Utrecht](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Utrecht \"Utrecht\") towards [Groningen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Groningen \"Groningen\") and [Leeuwarden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leeuwarden \"Leeuwarden\") in the North East.\n\nPassengers can also board direct international trains to Belgium (mostly Antwerp and Brussels) and [France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/France \"France\") (mostly Paris sometimes beyond) and London [UK](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UK \"UK\").\n\nFerries also leave daily from the [Europoort](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Europoort \"Europoort\") to [Kingston Upon Hull](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingston_Upon_Hull \"Kingston Upon Hull\") in the [UK](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UK \"UK\") daily, courtesy of [P&O Ferries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/P&O_Ferries \"P&O Ferries\"), as well as from [Hoek van Holland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hoek_van_Holland \"Hoek van Holland\") to [Harwich](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harwich \"Harwich\").\n\n### City level public transport\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Rotterdam&action=edit&section=38 \"Edit section: City level public transport\")]\n\nIn Rotterdam, public transport services are provided by trains operating [Nederlandse Spoorwegen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nederlandse_Spoorwegen \"Nederlandse Spoorwegen\") services between local stations. [Rotterdamse Elektrische Tram](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rotterdamse_Elektrische_Tram \"Rotterdamse Elektrische Tram\") operating metro, tram and bus services and bus services by [Arriva Netherlands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arriva \"Arriva\"), Connexxion, Qbuzz and [Transdev Netherlands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Connexxion \"Connexxion\").\n\nIn 1968, Rotterdam was the first Dutch city to operate a [metro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rapid_transit \"Rapid transit\") system. The metro system consists of three main lines, each of which has its own variants. The metro network has 78.3 km (48.7 mi) of rail tracks, and there are 70 stations, which makes it the largest of the Benelux. 5 lines operate the system: 3 lines (A, B and C) on the east–west line and two (D and E) on the north–south line. Opened in 2006, Line E (RandstadRail) connects Rotterdam with The Hague.\n\nThe [Rotterdam tramway network](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trams_in_Rotterdam \"Trams in Rotterdam\") offers 9 regular tram lines and 4 special tram lines with a total length of 93.4 km (58.0 mi).\n\nThe Waterbus network consists of seven lines. The mainline (Line 20) stretches from Rotterdam to Dordrecht. The ferry carries about 130 passengers, and there is space for 60 [bicycles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bicycle \"Bicycle\").\n\n## International relations\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Rotterdam&action=edit&section=39 \"Edit section: International relations\")]\n\nRotterdam has city and port connections throughout the world. In 2008, the city had 13 [sister cities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Twin_towns_and_sister_cities \"Twin towns and sister cities\"), 12 partner cities, and 4 sister ports.[[116]](#cite_note-brandstrategy-116) Since 2008, the City of Rotterdam does not forge new sister or partner connections. Sister and partner cities are not a priority in international relations.[[117]](#cite_note-117)\n\nOn 15 March 2017, the Turkish president expressed his wish that [Istanbul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Istanbul \"Istanbul\") should no longer be the twin town of Rotterdam. A speaker of the Rotterdam municipality then explained that the two cities have no official partnership. Both authorities do cooperate often.[[118]](#cite_note-118)\n\n### Twin towns – sister cities\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Rotterdam&action=edit&section=40 \"Edit section: Twin towns – sister cities\")]\n\nRotterdam is [twinned](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Twin_towns_and_sister_cities \"Twin towns and sister cities\") with:\n\n* [![United States](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/a/a4/Flag_of_the_United_States.svg/40px-Flag_of_the_United_States.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States \"United States\") [Baltimore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baltimore \"Baltimore\") (since 1985)[[116]](#cite_note-brandstrategy-116)[[119]](#cite_note-Vrijsen_2008-119)\n* \"Bulgaria\") [Burgas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Burgas \"Burgas\") (since 1976)[[116]](#cite_note-brandstrategy-116)[[119]](#cite_note-Vrijsen_2008-119)\n* \"Germany\") [Cologne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cologne \"Cologne\") (since 1958)[[116]](#cite_note-brandstrategy-116)[[119]](#cite_note-Vrijsen_2008-119)\n* \"Germany\") [Dresden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dresden \"Dresden\") (since 1988)[[116]](#cite_note-brandstrategy-116)[[119]](#cite_note-Vrijsen_2008-119)\n* [![Luxembourg](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/da/Flag_of_Luxembourg.svg/40px-Flag_of_Luxembourg.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luxembourg \"Luxembourg\") [Esch-sur-Alzette](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Esch-sur-Alzette \"Esch-sur-Alzette\") (since 1958)[[116]](#cite_note-brandstrategy-116)[[119]](#cite_note-Vrijsen_2008-119)\n* \"Poland\") [Gdańsk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gdańsk \"Gdańsk\") (since 1977)[[116]](#cite_note-brandstrategy-116)[[119]](#cite_note-Vrijsen_2008-119)\n* dish, *U.S.A. BICENTENNIAL TOWN OF ROTTERDAM, N.Y. 1776–1976*, with black coat of arms and cityscapes\n\nThe [town of Rotterdam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rotterdam_(town),_New_York \"Rotterdam (town), New York\"), located in the U.S. state of [New York](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_(state) \"New York (state)\"), was founded in 1661 by Dutch [settlers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Settler \"Settler\"), who named it after the city of Rotterdam in the [Netherlands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Netherlands \"Netherlands\"), where many immigrants last touched European grounds.[[120]](#cite_note-120) The town borders the [city](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Administrative_divisions_of_New_York#City \"Administrative divisions of New York\") of [Schenectady](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Schenectady,_New_York \"Schenectady, New York\"). Founded as a 'first class town' in 1942, Rotterdam has since adopted the Old World Rotterdam coat of arms along with the motto *Sterker door Strijd* (Stronger through Effort).[[121]](#cite_note-121)\n\n* [![Suriname](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/60/Flag_of_Suriname.svg/40px-Flag_of_Suriname.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suriname \"Suriname\") [Nieuw Rotterdam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nieuw_Rotterdam \"Nieuw Rotterdam\"), [Nickerie District](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nickerie_District \"Nickerie District\"), \"Miss Universe 1989\")\n\n## In popular culture\n\n[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Rotterdam&action=edit&section=45 \"Edit section: In popular culture\")]\n\n|  |\n| --- |\n| This section **needs [more citations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Verifiability \"Wikipedia:Verifiability\")**. Please help [improve this section](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special:EditPage/Rotterdam \"Special:EditPage/Rotterdam\") by [adding citations to reliable sources](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help:Referencing_for_beginners \"Help:Referencing for beginners\"). Unsourced material may be challenged and [removed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Verifiability#Burden_of_evidence \"Wikipedia:Verifiability\"). *(September 2017)* *([Learn how and when to remove this message](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help:Maintenance_template_removal \"Help:Maintenance template removal\"))* |\n\nRotterdam features in [Edgar Allan Poe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edgar_Allan_Poe \"Edgar Allan Poe\")'s short story \"[The Unparalleled Adventure of One Hans Pfaall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Unparalleled_Adventure_of_One_Hans_Pfaall \"The Unparalleled Adventure of One Hans Pfaall\")\" (1835).[[131]](#cite_note-131)\n\nRotterdam features in J.T. [Sheridan Le Fanu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sheridan_Le_Fanu \"Sheridan Le Fanu\")'s \"Strange Event in the Life of Schalken the Painter\" (1839).\n\nIn 1996, the [British](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Kingdom \"United Kingdom\") band [the Beautiful South](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Beautiful_South \"The Beautiful South\") recorded a song named after this region titled \"[Rotterdam (or Anywhere)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rotterdam_(or_Anywhere) \"Rotterdam (or Anywhere)\")\".[[132]](#cite_note-132)\n\nPart of [Jackie Chan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jackie_Chan \"Jackie Chan\")'s 1998 film *[Who Am I?](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Who_Am_I%3F_(1998_film) \"Who Am I? (1998 film)\")* is set in Rotterdam.\n\n*[Ender's Shadow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ender's_Shadow \"Ender's Shadow\")*, part of the series *[Ender's Game](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ender's_Game \"Ender's Game\"),* is partially set in Rotterdam.\n\nIn the 2004 video game *[Hitman: Contracts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hitman:_Contracts \"Hitman: Contracts\")*, the missions \"Rendezvous in Rotterdam\" and \"Deadly Cargo\" are both set in Rotterdam.\n\nThe [2017 Laurence Olivier Award](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017_Laurence_Olivier_Awards \"2017 Laurence Olivier Awards\")-winning play *[Rotterdam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rotterdam_(play) \"Rotterdam (play)\")*, written by [Jon Brittain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jon_Brittain \"Jon Brittain\"), is set in the city.\n\nIn *[Battlefield V](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battlefield_V \"Battlefield V\")*, this city appears in two launch maps, Rotterdam and Devastation, featuring battles between the [British Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Army \"British Army\") and the [Wehrmacht](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wehrmacht \"Wehrmacht\"). Historically, the [Witte Huis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Witte_Huis \"Witte Huis\") was almost untouched by the WWII bombings, and this building can be seen both in-game and in the real       "url": "https://www.rotterdamuas.com/go/living-in-rotterdam/financially-settling-in/costs-of-living-in-rotterdam",
      "title": "Costs of living in Rotterdam - Rotterdam University of Applied Sciences",
      "content": "Going out\n\nGoing out expenses may differ from one person to another. Drinking on a night out means paying €3,50 for a beer and €4,50 for a glass of wine. For culture lovers, a cinema ticket costs around  €11,50. A Cineville membership can offer you unlimited access to all independent film theatres. If you want to do a lot of fun things, a Rotterdam pass\\ will allow you to do so with a discount or even for free.\n\n\\Website only available in Dutch\n\nGroceries\n\nBuying bread, milk, eggs, cheese, pasta, vegetables and fruits will cost you approximately €250 per month.\n\nTransport [...] Transport\n\nThe Netherlands has a good public transportation network. A 2 hours ticket costs €4,50 . On average students spend around €60-€100 on public transport within the area of Rotterdam. This however depends on the distance that you need to travel as travelling between cities can become expensive. The great thing about the Netherlands though is that you can get around easily on a bike and this will save you a lot of money. Most students buy a second-hand bike from Marktplaats and Marketplace on Facebook, or get a monthly subscription to Swapfiets.\n\nMobile\n\nYou can choose between a prepaid SIM card and a contract. Students generally spend around €15 to €35 per month on data, calls and messaging. You can read more about obtaining a Dutch mobile number on our introduction page. [...] Menu \n\n Financially settling in \n  + Financially settling in\n  + Costs of living in Rotterdam\n  + Open a Dutch bank account\n  + My Study finance (DUO)\n  + Need financial help?\n Costs of living in Rotterdam\n\n Nederlands  \n\n# Costs of living in Rotterdam\n\nStudents living and studying in Rotterdam spend approximately €1000 to €1400 a month. It is needed to cover daily expenses, meals, accommodation costs, insurance etc. The amount varies based mainly on each student’s rent.\n\nAccommodation\n\nThe biggest part of your budget will usually go up to your rent. The amount for rent is between €475 and €1000 per month depending on the area, type of accommodation and what is included in the rent. You can find more information about accommodation on our housing page.\n\nGoing out",
      "score": 0.17899926,
      "raw_content": "[Menu](#) \n\n* [Financially settling in](/go/living-in-rotterdam/financially-settling-in/) \n  + [Financially settling in](/go/living-in-rotterdam/financially-settling-in/)\n  + [Costs of living in Rotterdam](/go/living-in-rotterdam/financially-settling-in/costs-of-living-in-rotterdam/)\n  + [Open a Dutch bank account](/go/living-in-rotterdam/financially-settling-in/open-a-dutch-bank-account/)\n  + [My Study finance (DUO)](/go/living-in-rotterdam/financially-settling-in/my-study-finance-duo/)\n  + [Need financial help?](/go/living-in-rotterdam/financially-settling-in/need-financial-help/)\n* [Costs of living in Rotterdam](/go/living-in-rotterdam/financially-settling-in/costs-of-living-in-rotterdam/)\n\n [Nederlands](https://www.hogeschoolrotterdam.nl/)  \n\n\n# Costs of living in Rotterdam\n\nStudents living and studying in Rotterdam spend approximately €1000 to €1400 a month. It is needed to cover daily expenses, meals, accommodation costs, insurance etc. The amount varies based mainly on each student’s rent.\n\n![](https://images.hogeschoolrotterdam.nl/Blob/6c9dbfe9f46a475dbe526ff96ef5a174/3d2b5db6ea1841a5960dd31a20575c2c.png?mode=crop&quality=90&format=jpg&width=1869&height=539)\n\n**Accommodation**\n\nThe biggest part of your budget will usually go up to your rent. The amount for rent is between €475 and €1000 per month depending on the area, type of accommodation and what is included in the rent. You can find more information about accommodation on our [housing page](/link/615189d1046f4d98903327c2e25bb763.aspx).\n\n**Going out**\n\nGoing out expenses may differ from one person to another. Drinking on a night out means paying €3,50 for a beer and €4,50 for a glass of wine. For culture lovers, a cinema ticket costs around  €11,50. A [Cineville](https://www.cineville.nl/en/card) membership can offer you unlimited access to all independent film theatres. If you want to do a lot of fun things, a [Rotterdam pass\\*](https://www.rotterdampas.nl/info-contact/hoe-werkt-het) will allow you to do so with a discount or even for free.\n\n\\*Website only available in Dutch\n\n**Groceries**\n\nBuying bread, milk, eggs, cheese, pasta, vegetables and fruits will cost you approximately €250 per month.\n\n**Transport**\n\nThe Netherlands has a good public transportation network. A 2 hours ticket costs €4,50 . On average students spend around €60-€100 on public transport within the area of Rotterdam. This however depends on the distance that you need to travel as travelling between cities can become expensive. The great thing about the Netherlands though is that you can get around easily on a bike and this will save you a lot of money. Most students buy a second-hand bike from [Marktplaats](https://www.marktplaats.nl/) and Marketplace on Facebook, or get a monthly subscription to [Swapfiets](https://swapfiets.nl/en).\n\n**Mobile**\n\nYou can choose between a prepaid SIM card and a contract. Students generally spend around €15 to €35 per month on data, calls and messaging. You can read more about obtaining a Dutch mobile number on our [introduction page](/link/b972dce9fff342ae8ea61e755b1674b5.aspx).\n\n**Insurance**\n\nThe type of healthcare insurance you need depends on your personal situation. A private student insurance costs between €60-€140 per month. More information about health insurance can be found on our [health insurance page.](/link/63fc52ade106416f8bfa7216347c67a3.aspx)\n\n**Sports**\n\nOn average, a gym membership fluctuates between €25 to €50 per month. RUAS students can also use the Erasmus Sports facilities, more information about their packages and fees can be found on [their website](https://erasmussport.nl/).\n\n1. [Home](https://www.rotterdamuas.com/)\n\nShare this page   [Share on LinkedIn](http://www.linkedin.com/shareArticle?mini=true&url=https://www.rotterdamuas.com/go/living-in-rotterdam/financially-settling-in/costs-of-living-in-rotterdam&title=Interesting+page+about+%22Costs+of+living+in+Rotterdam%22+on+the+Rotterdam+University+of+Applied+Sciences+website.)   [Share on Twitter](http://twitter.com/share?count=horizontal&original_referer=https://www.rotterdamuas.com/go/living-in-rotterdam/financially-settling-in/costs-of-living-in-rotterdam&text=Interesting+page+about+%22Costs+of+living+in+Rotterdam%22+on+the+Rotterdam+University+of+Applied+Sciences+website.&url=https://www.rotterdamuas.com/go/living-in-rotterdam/financially-settling-in/costs-of-living-in-rotterdam)   [Share on Facebook](http://www.facebook.com/sharer.php?u=https://www.rotterdamuas.com/go/living-in-rotterdam/financially-settling-in/costs-of-living-in-rotterdam)   [Email this page](mailto:?subject=Interesting+page+about+%22Costs+of+living+in+Rotterdam%22+on+the+Rotterdam+University+of+Applied+Sciences+website.&body=https://www.rotterdamuas.com/go/living-in-rotterdam/financially-settling-in/costs-of-living-in-rotterdam)   [Print this page](javascript:print())\n\n## Log in\n\n[### HINT\n\nHINT is the intranet for students and staff.](http://hint.hro.nl/)\n\n[### My RUAS\n\nCheck your enrolment status through my.rotterdamuas.com. Students can check their calendar, results and more on my.rotterdamuas.com.](http://my.rotterdamuas.com/)\n\n[### InTouch\n\nDo you have an internship/placement​, thesis or research project? Publish it in InTouch.](http://intouch.hr.nl/)\n\n[Close](#)\n\n ",

      "title": "Indians in Netherlands – My experiences",
      "content": "8. Electronics store: media markt,coolbuy.com .…look for Sale. Also check the price in mediamarkt.nl. Products are often much cheaper there. You can even order it to the nearest store at no additional shipping cost and pick it up from there a day or 2 later.\n9. Petty Medicines: Etos\n10. Medical store: any ‘Apotheek’, but unless it is over the counter medicine, you will need prescription from a Dutch doctor.\n11. Post office: Generally a separate counter in ‘Bruna’ book store.\n12. If you did not buy a mobile SIM card at the airport, you can buy it at the train station information/currency exchange desk. Some phone shops in the Centrum will also sell. [...] 1. Upon landing in Netherlands, you can buy pre-paid SIM card at the airport. There are many options. It costs usually cost €5 to €15 to buy the SIM often with some currency already loaded. Compare and then buy. Check Lebara, AH mobile, KPN, Vodafone etc. Lyca is quite popular as well but people often complain that it doesn’t work when you travel to Germany, Swiss, France etc.\n2. If you are landing in Schipol airport, the train station is within the airport. Make sure your destination trainstation has a lift/escalator from the platform to the transit point. Else you will have to carry your luggage up/down the stairs. Normally taxis/city-bus are available outside the train stations. [...] 5. Which telecom operator should I choose for buying a SIM card. I’m not sure if I would be having complimentary WIFI in my accommodation, but still I would prefer a plan with data/voice benefits both.",
      "score": 0.1406077,
      "raw_content": "[Skip to content](#main \"Skip to content\")\n\n\n[Indians in Netherlands](https://indiansinnetherlands.com/ \"Indians in Netherlands\")   \n My experiences\n\n# Knowing Netherlands\n\nI came to Netherlands towards the end of 2009. Since then, I have seen the Indian crowd only expand…exponentially would not be a wrong term to use! Primarily I see people coming for work, graduation studies, post-graduation Masters, Research (PhD) and tourism…especially parents. BTW I’m here for work.\n\nGiving some tips to a colleague new to Netherlands gave me a thought to blog it.\n\n.\n\nBefore travel – Preparation and luggage:\n\n1. Primary language here is Dutch. But most people in Netherlands are able to speak English.\n2. If you are browsing to find information and everything is in English letters but strange words, it is probably because it is Dutch language. Download Google Chrome. It will ask you if you want to translate Dutch to (decent enough) English. City center is called ‘Centrum’\n3. Most of Netherlands is safe and crime rate is very low. However petty theft and house robbery does occur. So don’t carry too much jewelry 🙂\n4. Mid Oct thru early March is cold. Outside temperature will likely be in and around 0 degree centigrade. If you want to know how it feels, just keep your hands inside a fridge freezer for 1 minute! Carry a good woolen gloves, woolen cap that covers even your ears, a good winter jacket and thermal wear. For April thru September, it is better to carry a good warm jacket anyway. It could be very warm or chilly at random. Google “weather in <>” to know the current weather conditions. The forecast is fairly accurate for upto a week ahead….only that I think it rains less than what they forecast.\n5. Unless it is arranged for you, carry enough ready to eat food for at least 1 day. Not only that you will likely be tired and hungry, depending on the day/time you land, no shops might be open!\n6. Unlike India, 95% of the shops (excluding restaurants) close by 6:00PM. On Sunday, except Centrum almost all shops will be closed. Even the few restaurants that open will close early. (One exception is Albert Heijn grocery store. They are open till 10:00PM Mon-Sat and also few hours on Sunday in most cities. Go to their website to find a store near you).\n7. If you want to cook yourself, carry your pressure cooker (carry spare gasket and safety valve), mixer (mixers here do not scale up to Indian needs of grinding). You may want to carry home made ‘saaru’ and ‘sambar’ powder. However MTR is available in Indian shops here.\n8. Many houses here have induction stove only. So your cooking vessels/cooker need to be compatible.\n9. The electric plug point shape is different from India. Carry a couple of European pin to Indian pin converters. It should cost within Rs100/- in India while it could cost upto €10 euros to buy it here.\n10. Voltage/frequency is 220V/50Hz. So your mixer, laptop will work here…you only need the European pin to Indian pin converter.\n11. Mobile is GSM based. So if your mobile is a GSM phone, it will work here.\n12. Turn on International roaming and topup with enough currency. Airtel e-SIM also works well.\n13. Your WhatsApp number will continue to work from here. Even if you change to Netherlands SIM, you have the option to continue your India WhatsApp number. This might be handy especially when you are coming for short-term.\n14. Carry needed medication. You will not get medicines unless you have a prescription from a Dutch doctor. Only exception is Paracetamol and other petty medicines. So carry needed medication sufficient for at least 2 months.\n15. If you wear spectacles, carry a spare. It will be expensive here and takes them 2 weeks to deliver.\n16. Most likely your visa will be “Knowledge Work Permit” (also called highly skilled migrant or Kennismigrant) or ICT (Intra company transfer). If so, your spouse (dependent) can also work any job.\n17. Carry your original driving license . There is no need for IDP since Indian license is in English. You are allowed to drive with India license for 6 months after your arrival.\n18. If you have a small child, carry the vaccination and growth report/summary till date. While this is not mandatory, it will be handy in determining the remaining vaccinations for your child.\n19. If you have a child and need English medium school, you need to find an[‘International school’](https://indiansinnetherlands.com/my-experiences/schooling-in-netherlands/ \"Schooling from 0 to 18yrs\")first and based on that decide your housing. Contact the school immediately (even before coming to Netherlands) to get advice on vacancy.\n20. Check your office dress code. Suit might be preferred/needed.\n21. A good fit formal pants could be difficult to find here since shops do not adjust the length.\n22. Due to weather conditions, clothes do not dry in the Sun. You need to wash and dry in a washing machine once in 3 to 7 days. So I suggest you carry extra sets of clothes.\n23. For the India to Netherlands flight journey, ideally you will need very little money – to cover for any food/shopping need during your stopover. But carry at least €200 to €300 more just in case something goes wrong midway forcing longer stopover (snow, fog, Iceland volcano, strike etc). It is best to carry in Euro, US Dollar or Pounds. BEWARE……INDIAN RUPEES MAY NOT BE ACCEPTED OUTSIDE INDIA even in currency exchange centers.\n24. Most airlines allow 1 check-in baggage of 23 Kgs. A few allow 30 Kgs (Etihad, Emirates etc).\n25. If you are migrating for employment, you are likely to be eligible for 30% ruling. This means first 30% of your gross is tax free. This is a significant financial advantage. Ask your employer for it. For more details [click here.](https://indiansinnetherlands.com/money-matters/take-home-and-taxes/ \"Take home, taxes and benefits\")\n26. Open a NRE bank account when you are in India. Opening after coming here, is a long and painful process. Also get the internet banking userid/password so that you can make all transactions via the internet and hassle free.\n27. Consider giving “Power of Attorney” to someone close to you. Understand the risks before you do it.\n28. Join the relevant [facebook groups](https://indiansinnetherlands.com/my-experiences/facebook-groups/ \"Facebook groups\")….do not hesitate to ask your question.\n29. If you are bringing expensive items like gold, laptop, mobile etc make sure it is unpacked and looks like it is for personal use. Else you could be stopped by customs at the airport and asked to pay 21% VAT and also may be a fine.\n\nDay 0:\n\n1. Upon landing in Netherlands, you can buy pre-paid SIM card at the airport. There are many options. It costs usually cost €5 to €15 to buy the SIM often with some currency already loaded. Compare and then buy. Check Lebara, AH mobile, KPN, Vodafone etc. Lyca is quite popular as well but people often complain that it doesn’t work when you travel to Germany, Swiss, France etc.\n2. If you are landing in Schipol airport, the train station is within the airport. Make sure your destination [train](http://www.ns.nl/en/travellers/home)station has a lift/escalator from the platform to the transit point. Else you will have to carry your luggage up/down the stairs. Normally taxis/city-bus are available outside the train stations.\n3. If train is not an option for you, ‘airport shuttle’ is the next cheapest followed by taxi. Take official taxi only (and not someone who is trying to sell you a taxi inside the airport) to avoid paying much more than official rates. Note: Car seat for kids is not mandatory for airport shuttle and taxis.\n\nWeek 1:\n\n1. Grocery stores: Albert Heijn (also Lidel, C1000, Jumbo). You will get tons of varieties. Some items specific to Indian taste are:\n   * Butter – ‘Roombotter – Ongezouten’,\n   * Milk – melk\n   * Curds – ‘Bio Garde, STAND Naturel’ (ask for yogurt section),\n   * buttermilk – ‘Karnemelk’,\n2. If you are really out of stuff late at night, you can go to ‘Night Shop’ or ‘Albert Heign to go’ shop that are open till late in the night. However you will get only limited stuff and is not a substitute for your grocery shopping. Also, these shops are more expensive than regular grocery shops. Night Shop will be open till about 1.00AM.\n3. Clothes store: ‘C&A’, Hema, PRIMARK – cheap and good. Often there will be discount ‘Sale’. So buy only what you need and wait for the Sale weeks for the rest.\n4. Miscellaneous store: Action, Kruidvat, Zeeman. You will get stationery, mug, bucket, DVDs, batteries, shopping cart, rice cooker etc etc etc.\n5. Footware store: van Haren is cheap and good….again keep ‘Sale’ weeks in mind.\n6. Indian store: Most cities have Indian stores where you get almost all of your day to day needs as if in India. You will get ready to eat, Maggi, soan pappdi, grated coconut…everything! If you have a stove and a microwave in your accommodation, life is easy here!\n7. Indian store: Other than Indian stores, Chinese, Turkish and international stores also store Indian food items (like dals, ready chapatti, pudina, palak etc)\n8. Electronics store: media markt,coolbuy.com .…look for Sale. Also check the price in mediamarkt.nl. Products are often much cheaper there. You can even order it to the nearest store at no additional shipping cost and pick it up from there a day or 2 later.\n9. Petty Medicines: Etos\n10. Medical store: any ‘Apotheek’, but unless it is over the counter medicine, you will need prescription from a Dutch doctor.\n11. Post office: Generally a separate counter in ‘Bruna’ book store.\n12. If you did not buy a mobile SIM card at the airport, you can buy it at the train station information/currency exchange desk. Some phone shops in the Centrum will also sell.\n13. A shopping cart will cost you €15/-. But worth the buy since it will last for years and will relieve you the pain of carrying stuff in hand. With a shopping cart, you fill it with your grocery and drag it on its wheels to your home.\n14. To travel by train, it is good to know the [basic ticketing](https://indiansinnetherlands.com/travel/public-transport-and-ov-chipkaart/ \"Public transport and OV chipkaart\")system. Go to [http://www.ns.nl](http://www.ns.nl/) for timetable, ticket price. Click English if you prefer information in English. Trains have a confusing variety of discounts, ticketing modes and eligibility criteria for these. If you are confused, go to the ticket counter at the station and ask them what is best for your situation. However you will pay 50cents extra per ticket if you buy tickets at the counter instead of the vending machine.\n15. For door-to-door travel planning using [public transport](https://indiansinnetherlands.com/travel/public-transport-and-ov-chipkaart/ \"Public transport and OV chipkaart\"), go to <http://www.9292.nl/>. For English, click the UK flag. If you provide your home address and your destination address, it will tell you the options you have for travel.\n16. Once you get your insurance letter from your insurance company, you can register yourself with a Doctor. The healthcare system is  different and important to know. Read my [other post](https://indiansinnetherlands.com/my-experiences/the-medical-system/ \"The medical system\") for details.\n17. Jewelry: If you brought gold jewelry, keep them in a locker facility. Since 2024, theft in Indian houses  has become a common phenomenon.\n\n```\n[adsense]\n```\n\nMonth 1\n\n1. ABN-AMRO bank has English online banking. Most others have only Dutch. While it is not mandatory, you may still be asked for BSN (aadhar card equivalent) to open the account. How to apply for a BSN and the time it takes is explained [here](https://indiansinnetherlands.com/my-experiences/time-it-takes/ \"Paper work – time it takes\"). Internet based banks like Bunq, Revolut etc are also gaining popularity and have apps in English.\n2. Other than health insurance, it is strongly advised to take 2 more types of insurance. Personal Insurance- in case you/family damage someone else’s property by mistake. Theft Insurance – to cover your house hold items in case of theft. ABN-AMRO also offers both and cost very nominal amount.\n3. Ebay of Holland: – <http://www.marktplaats.nl/> ; [http://www.tweakers.net](http://www.tweakers.net/)\n4. Buy a cycle. New one will be very expensive. You should easily be able to find a good used (second hand) cycle. Do not buy from unknown people who look like thugs and are ready to sell at very cheap price. Most likely these are stolen cycles and police might question you. Look for something within known circles. There are second hand shops but cost much more than private owners.\n5. If your spouse is not working, he/she can apply for tax return in the local tax office. After first 6 months of living in Holland, non-working spouse will get a monthly tax return of  aprox €80/month.\n6. If you have child(ren) you can apply for child benefit with SVB. You will get about € 150 per quarter if you have 1 child and more if you have more. Google SVB for more info.\n7. If you have a small child, you will need a stroller (buggy). They are available in different sizes. Big and bulky ones look very fancy and attractive to buy. But buy a small handy stroller. Primary reason is, if you are new to Europe, you will probably travel by public transport or to other Euorpean countries. Bulky ones are really a nuisance to carry around and handle in transport vehicles and in sightseeing areas. They are very heavy as well.\n8. If both you and your spouse are working and you need a day care for your child, you can get partial day-care fee refund from the government.  Depending on the city you live in, it may be easy to extremely difficult to get a day care for your child. Try nearby smaller cities if getting it in your city is difficult.\n9. In spite of the luxuries, you will need friends….especially for a housewife/kids. The best way to make friends is to invite someone who you meet with similar situation as you to your home formally for tea/dinner (and not just “come home sometime”…this will never work). Don’t wait for the other party to initiate. Also, long timers in NL would already have their circle. So best bet is to make your own group of recently arrived.\n10. There are quite a few Indians associations that conduct functions on account of Diwali, Ugadi, Vishu, Pongal etc. Google and facebook should help you to find one: \n    * kannada: [**shrigandha**.**nl**](http://www.shrigandha.nl/)\n    * Telugu: <https://www.facebook.com/tanetherlands>\n    * Diwali celebration in Amsterdam: <http://www.diwalifestival.nl/nl/route/4-route>\n11. You are allowed to drive in Netherlands with a foreign license for first 6 months after your arrival.\n12. If your 30% ruling is approved, you can [exchange your Indian license](https://indiansinnetherlands.com/travel/car-and-driving/ \"car and driving\") for a Dutch license paying a nominal fee at your local town hall (for a fee of about €50). Spouse of a 30% ruling holder is also eligible for this exchange. For further details on DL: [http://www.expatica.com/nl/leisure/travel\\_tourism/how-to-get-a-dutch-drivers-licence-1672.html.](http://www.expatica.com/nl/leisure/travel_tourism/how-to-get-a-dutch-drivers-licence-1672.html) You can collect your Indian license back from VFS India center about 6 months after the exchange.\n13. Spouse of a Knowledge Work Permit holder is not eligible for 30% ruling if he/she gets a job  after coming to Netherlands as a dependent. However many have received the 30% ruling if they got employed within 3 months of coming to Netherlands.\n14. Changing jobs:\n    * If you are on Knowledge Work Permit, it is allowed to change your employer.\n    * If you are on ICT visa it is possible to change the employer if the new employer is authorized to process KWP visa. Google for details.\n15. You could get calls like “We are calling from IND. Your visa is illegal…your records are illegal. You urgently need to transfer money to correct it” etc. These are fake calls. If anyone calls you, ask them to talk in Dutch, or you say you will call back to IND to check the validity of the call. They will hang up and stop bothering you.\n16. Ryanair, Wizz air, Transavia are good low cost airlines for travel within Europe if planned at least 4 weeks in advance. Use the ‘Skyscanner’ mobile app to plan your vacation. It lists in the order of ticket cost to all destinations from a given source for a given period.\n17. If you have any grievances when living in The Netherlands (example: landlord issues, dispute with a business etc), you can approach the Indian Embassy for help. They are often very eager to help out.\n18. Probably you are already looking forward to plan your next trip…[.click here](http://sightseeingineurope.2migrate.com) for a list of places to visit in Netherlands and Europe.\n19. Popular cinema complex is Pathe: [http://www.pathe.nl](http://www.pathe.nl/)\n20. If you have a lot of junk to throw away:\n    * for daily rubbish, you can collect them in a black dustbin bag (buy in grocery store) and keep them on the footpath weekly on a designated day for your area.\n    * for clothes there is a separate dustbin. You can find these near some schools and daycares\n    * to throw plastic waste and bottles, dustbins will be near shopping centers\n    * for batteries disposal, you can check for a waste bin in your office or go to electric shops like media markt for example.\n    * for anything and everything, go to Millieustraat. It is the city waste collection center. Some cities charge you a nominal fee and some do not.\n21. To meet like minded groups/people: <http://www.meetup.com/find/>\n22. To learn Dutch on your own:\n    * Radio: <http://tunein.com/topic/?TopicId=37787463>\n    * Text: <http://www.dutchgrammar.com/>\n23. If you are a vegetarian, you will most likely develop Vitamin D deficiency because of limited exposure to sunlight especially in the winter months. Consume Vitamin D supplement tablets. This is available without prescription at any medical store.\n24. This point is a little too far away for most, but you must know if you are working in NL. For every year of working in NL, you are eligible for 2% of AOW value as monthly pension after you reach retirement age (about 67). The AOW value is determined by SVB every year depending on inflation etc. In 2013, its value is about €725.00. So before leaving NL for good, call SVB and ask for a statement. Then call them when you are 66 to arrange for the pension!! 🙂\n25. Over and above the previous point, your employer might subscribe to additional pension scheme. When leaving NL, you can either get this transferred to a similar policy in India or just leave it as is and check back with them when you are 66 years old!\n26. File your income tax in India even if you have 0 income. If you stay long term, it will be easier to move your assets from India to here.\n\nDisclaimer: This is my best effort and personal opinion only. No responsibility will be taken for any inaccuracy.\n\n## 373 thoughts on “Knowing Netherlands”\n\n1. ![](https://secure.gravatar.com/avatar/7a32b7f56b4eca73372746863ed6ad15?s=30&r=g)Harish Kumar  \n\n   Nice work Mr.Madhu! Overall, the pay is decent enough (your house rent is what you need to control for better savings, the rest are small costs). Do not forget to apply for the 30% ruling within 3 months of your arrival. Also make sure you apply for the non-working spouse tax return and the child benefit explained in my blog.  \n       2. Most Indians live in Amstelveen. But this area is very expensive. Almere is an alternative. Indian population is growing here and also has international school. Stay close to the station.  \n       3. Normally yes. Unless you/family are at health risk of specs, physiotherapy etc, you will not need additional insurance. Dental insurance is something you need to consider depending on the health of your teeth 🙂  \n       4. Generally 4000 to 6000 per year. There are others that cost 15,000+. Only people whose companies sponsor full fees send their children to these schools. Note: If you have 30% ruling, your employer can deduct the fees from your gross thereby making it non taxable. Admission is anytime of the year – the day your child turns 4. Both of you will have to go back to India. So the work around is, ask your wife company to process her own MVV when she gets a job. Later whenever you leave your job, you can forfeit your MVV and register yourself in the IND as her dependent! Note that if you do not get a job within 3 months of your last job, you will lose your 30% ruling!\n\n       [Reply](https://indiansinnetherlands.com/?replytocom=30146#respond) ↓\n60. ![](https://secure.gravatar.com/avatar/985827782d181867fd4c56e54bd55332?s=30&r=g)Vamsee  \n\n    Dear Sir,\n\n    Thank you for such a useful site. If they go to International school (English medium), the school fees will be about 7000€ per child per year while a Dutch or Dual language school (called twetaligOnderwijs(TTO)) is almost free. Engineering is easily possible from International school. Medicine is also possible via International school but is academically a more difficult/longer route than if they go to Dutch/TTO. Note that for the 13yr old, not all Dutch schools might be open to accept admission because of the language gap.\n\n        [Reply](https://indiansinnetherlands.com/?replytocom=176120#respond) ↓\n166. ![](https://secure.gravatar.com/avatar/be9665ffadf995a24931e857ede17db2?s=30&r=g)Sudip  \n\n     Hello Madhu, Happy New Year to you.\n\n     I have one question if you can please help – Is the work permit valid for 5 years (under MVV) extendable after 5 years?       "url": "https://www.rotterdam.info/en/visit/good-to-know/travelling-in-rotterdam",
      "title": "Travelling in Rotterdam",
      "content": "### Bus\n\nThe RET provides several bus routes which connect the centre with several city districts, neighbourhoods and the greater metropolitan area. Check the RET website for further information about routes and stops.\n\n### How to pay in public transportation [...] RET 1 day: on sale at Rotterdam Tourist Information (Central Station and Hoek van Holland), RET service stores and service points. Full fare € 12.00 / RET 1 Day Ticket Child € 6.00.\n RET 2-hour : on sale and rechargeable at sales and recharge points in metro stations, on sale from bus driver (pay only with pin), in streetcars (pay both pin and cash), Rotterdam Tourist Information (Central Station and Hoek van Holland). € 5.50.\n RET Tourist Day Ticket: 1 day of unlimited travel throughout South Holland (bus, streetcar, metro, waterbus) for € 17.50: on sale at Rotterdam Tourist Information (Central Station and Hoek van Holland), RET service stores and service points and the Vending & charging machines in metro stations. [...] ### Metro\n\nThe metro is a fast and efficient way to travel through Rotterdam and the surrounding region. There are five lines, marked with the letters A through E, that connect different parts of the city and region. Station Beurs is the main hub, where all subways converge. Line E (RandstadRail) connects Rotterdam to The Hague, while line B travels from Nesselande to Hoek van Holland Haven. Check the RET website for further information about routes and stops.\n\n### Tram\n\nThe trams are ideal for a ride through the city center and connect popular destinations. Many lines run through the city and converge at Rotterdam Central Station. This makes the station the perfect boarding point to take the tram. Check the RET website for further information about routes and stops.\n\n### Bus",
      "score": 0.12130033,
      "raw_content": "[Jump to the content](#a11y-main)[Jump to the footer](#a11y-footer)\n\n\n[Go to homepageVisitRotterdam](/en/visit \"Go to homepage\")\n\n[My List0](/en/visit/my-list)[Rotterdam City Card](/en/visit/rotterdam-city-card \"Rotterdam City Card\")[Search through our website](/en/visit/search)en\n\n\n\n# Travelling in Rotterdam\n\nGetting around Rotterdam is easy. Whether you prefer travelling by public transport, bicycle, car or on foot, the city and its surroundings are easy to explore. As home to Europe's largest port, Rotterdam also offers a unique and popular way to discover the city from the water.\n\n[Unlimited public transport with the Rotterdam City Card](/en/visit/rotterdam-city-card)\n\n![](https://rotterdampartners-rp-website-p01.s3.eu-central-1.amazonaws.com/small_Zuidplein_metrostation_metro_Guido_Pijper_00b4d7c849.webp)\n\n## Public Transport\n\nRotterdam has an excellent public transport network, provided by the RET. You can use the bus, tram and metro to get almost anywhere in the city and region. This makes it easy to explore the city, both for short and longer distances.\n\n### **Metro**\n\nThe metro is a fast and efficient way to travel through Rotterdam and the surrounding region. There are five lines, marked with the letters A through E, that connect different parts of the city and region. Station Beurs is the main hub, where all subways converge. Line E (RandstadRail) connects Rotterdam to The Hague, while line B travels from Nesselande to Hoek van Holland Haven. Check the [RET website](https://www.ret.nl/en/home/travelling-with-the-ret/maps.html) for further information about routes and stops.\n\n### **Tram**\n\nThe trams are ideal for a ride through the city center and connect popular destinations. Many lines run through the city and converge at Rotterdam Central Station. This makes the station the perfect boarding point to take the tram. Check the [RET website](https://www.ret.nl/en/home/travelling-with-the-ret/maps.html) for further information about routes and stops.\n\n### **Bus**\n\nThe RET provides several bus routes which connect the centre with several city districts, neighbourhoods and the greater metropolitan area. Check the [RET website](https://www.ret.nl/en/home/travelling-with-the-ret/maps.html) for further information about routes and stops.\n\n### **How to pay in public transportation**\n\nWith the [Rotterdam City Card](https://www.rotterdam.info/en/visit/city-card-rotterdam), you can travel unlimited on RET public transport for 1, 2, 3, 4 or 5 days. In addition, you'll enjoy a 25% discount or more at attractions, museums, tours, and restaurants.  \n  \n**OVpay**  \nYou can also travel on public transport without the Rotterdam City Card. Simply pay contactless with your debit card, credit card or mobile phone using [OVpay](https://www.ovpay.nl/hoe-het-werkt). OVpay also works with many international debit and credit cards, provided they are issued by Maestro, V PAY, Mastercard or Visa. Watch the instruction video for more information.\n\nPlease note: In many cases, travelling with OVpay is more expensive than unlimited travel with the Rotterdam City Card. You can also travel with an OV-chipkaart or a mobile ticket in the [RET app](https://www.ret.nl/home/reizen/app.html). The RET app lets you buy tickets for the metro, tram and bus directly on your phone.\n\n**You can also buy various public transport cards (fares 2026):**\n\n* RET 1 day: on sale at [Rotterdam Tourist Information](https://www.rotterdam.info/en/visit/good-to-know/rotterdam-tourist-information-desks) ([Central Station](https://www.rotterdam.info/en/visit/finder-locations/rotterdam-tourist-information-rotterdam-centraal) and [Hoek van Holland](https://www.rotterdam.info/en/visit/finder-locations/rotterdam-tourist-information-hoek-van-holland)), RET service stores and service points. Full fare € 12.00 / RET 1 Day Ticket Child € 6.00.\n* RET 2-hour : on sale and rechargeable at sales and recharge points in metro stations, on sale from bus driver (pay only with pin), in streetcars (pay both pin and cash), Rotterdam Tourist Information (Central Station and Hoek van Holland). € 5.50.\n* RET Tourist Day Ticket: 1 day of unlimited travel throughout South Holland (bus, streetcar, metro, waterbus) for € 17.50: on sale at Rotterdam Tourist Information (Central Station and Hoek van Holland), RET service stores and service points and the Vending & charging machines in metro stations.\n\nTo be able to view this content you need to accept the cookies that come from this third party.\n\nOVpay - How it works?\n\n## Transport by water\n\nRotterdam offers several unique ways to explore the city by water. The Waterbus and Watertaxi are popular options for tourists and locals alike.\n\n### Waterbus\n\nThe Waterbus is the largest fast ferry service in the Rotterdam and Dordrecht region, offering a regular timetable along several stops on the river. From the Rotterdam Erasmus Bridge stop (north side), Line 20 departs towards Dordrecht (Merwekade), stopping at Krimpen aan den IJssel, Ridderkerk, Alblasserdam, Hendrik-Ido-Ambacht and Papendrecht. From Dordrecht, there are connections to Zwijndrecht and the Biesbosch National Park. The Waterbus operates on both a summer and winter timetable.\n\nFor a direct connection to Kinderdijk, take the [WaterShuttle](https://www.rotterdam.info/en/visit/finder-locations/kinderdijk-met-de-watershuttle). This ferry departs from Willemskade in Rotterdam and takes you directly to Kinderdijk in around 30 minutes.\n\n### **Watertaxi & veerpont**\n\nNothing is more spectacular than a ride on a fast [Watertaxi](https://www.watertaxirotterdam.nl/steigerlocaties). With some 50 stops, it is also an easy way to get from A to B. The stops are in Rotterdam and Schiedam, and the sailing area is divided into three fare zones: East, West and Center. In and between these zones, there are fixed fare prices per person. The eight- and twelve-seater watertaxis are available on demand. You can make a reservation via the app, website or by phone.\n\nOn weekends, you can also enjoy a special experience with the Water Taxi: [the Circle Line](https://www.watertaxirotterdam.nl/veerdiensten). This route offers passengers a scenic trip along Rotterdam's beautiful waterways. It is a popular option for both tourists and locals looking for a unique way to spend their weekend.\n\nThe [ferry services](https://www.watertaxirotterdam.nl/veerdiensten) operated by the Watertaxi are:\n\n* in zone West, this is the route RDM/Heijplaat - Marconistraat /M4H\n* in zone Center Charloisse Hoofd - Katendrechtse Hoofd\n* in zone East Plantagelaan - Piekstraat\n\nVMR provides the ferry between [Maassluis and Rozenburg](https://veermaassluisrozenburg.nl/) and deploys 2 ferries for this purpose:\n\n* Ferry Blankenburg: for cars, trucks, buses, motorbikes, heavy traffic, bicycles and walkers.\n* Ferry Jorina: for cyclists and pedestrians only.\n\n[Hoeksveer](https://www.rotterdam.info/nl/visit/finder-locations/hoeksveer) provides the connection over water between [Hoek van Holland](https://www.rotterdam.info/en/visit/guide/discover-hoek-van-holland) and Maasvlakte 2:\n\n* The small ferry operates from May to October and is only for cyclists and walkers, connecting (inter)national long-distance routes.\n\n![Watertaxi sailing the river Maas, with the Erasmus bridge and part of the Wilhelminapier skyline visible.](https://rotterdampartners-rp-website-p01.s3.eu-central-1.amazonaws.com/small_Watertaxi_en_skyline_Iris_van_den_Broek_e7c698e35a.webp)Iris van den Broek\n\nThe water taxis take you to 50 stops in Rotterdam and Schiedam\n\n## Taxi\n\nRotterdam has a good network of taxis, ideal for when you want to travel quickly from one place to another. Here are some taxi providers:\n\n* **Rotterdamse Taxi Centrale (RTC)**: 010 462 60 60\n* **Rotterdam Airport Taxi**: 010 262 04 06\n* **Taxi St-Job**: 010 425 70 00\n* **Stadstaxi Rotterdam**: 010 818 28 23\n* **Fietstaxi (a sort of covered bicycle)** 010 411 02 22 of 06 15295901\n\nFor more information on the requirements taxis must meet and where to find them, you can visit the Municipality of Rotterdam’s [website](https://www.rotterdam.nl/taxivervoer).\n\n## Find the nearest taxi rank here\n\nTo be able to view this content you need to accept the cookies that come from this third party.\n\n## Shared Mobility and Rental\n\n[Shared Transport](https://www.rotterdam.nl/deelvervoer) or Shared Mobility is a flexible and economical way of travelling, where you do not need your own means of transport. Instead, you only pay for the use of a shared vehicle, such as a shared scooter, shared bike or shared car, and for the number of kilometres you cover. When you reach your destination or one of the many Shared Mobility Hubs, you park the vehicle so that the next user can benefit from it again. This makes it ideal for short trips around town or for longer distances within the region. For greater flexibility, you can also rent a car or scooter from various rental companies, which give you the freedom to explore the city or surrounding area at your own pace.\n\n## Plan your trip\n\nPlanning your route within Rotterdam by public transport, car, bike or on foot? Check [Rotterdam Bereikbaar](https://www.rotterdam.nl/rotterdam-bereikbaar). This page will also keep you informed about traffic diversions and works in Rotterdam.\n\n## Information about roadworks & events in Rotterdam\n\nTo be able to view this content you need to accept the cookies that come from this third party.\n\n## Where to Park in Rotterdam\n\nPlanning a visit to Rotterdam and traveling by car? Park easily and affordably by leaving your car at a Park+Ride facility or in a parking garage! Want to secure your parking spot? Make a reservation, it's easy and more affordable.\n\n![Een foto van de een auto die uit de parkeergarage van het Museumpark komt](https://rotterdampartners-rp-website-p01.s3.eu-central-1.amazonaws.com/small_Parkeren_Parkeergarage_Museumpark_Eric_Fecken_8a992b64f0.webp)\n\n ",
